# CLAUDE.md

This file provides guidance to Claude Code (claude.ai/code) when working with code in this repository.

## Overview

Pulse is a single-file, zero-dependency HTML application — the entire app (HTML, CSS, JavaScript) lives in `pulse.html`. There is no build step and no package manager. It's a local-first programme dashboard, organized into four top-level modes (see "Modes" below): **Planning** (workstreams, scope items, milestones — a status board), **Review** (dated, per-workstream review cycles that require every item to be confirmed before completing), **Dashboard** (a read-only health rollup + attention-needed feed), and **Admin** (the category lookup table that seeds a new item's milestone checklist). It's also an installable PWA (manifest, service worker, icons), and has the same data-durability feature set as this author's other single-file apps (PromptLab, mytasks): JSON export/import, rolling local backups, deletion tombstones with undo, and optional File System Access API file sync — see "Data durability" below.

## Running it

A plain `file://` open of `pulse.html` works fine for quick checks, but PWA install/offline support requires serving it over http — browsers refuse to register a service worker for `file://` pages. `./start-pulse.command` serves the directory on `http://127.0.0.1:8936` and opens it in Chrome/Edge (Safari can't install this kind of PWA on macOS). `./stop-pulse.command` kills the server.

## Tests

There's no lint config, but there is a test suite at `tests/`: extract the inline `<script>` block, mock just enough of the DOM (`document.getElementById` returning cached fake elements with a settable `innerHTML`/`value`, plus `localStorage`, `navigator`, `window`, `location`, `Blob`/`URL` for the export/download path) and `eval()` the real code in a headless JS engine (`osascript -l JavaScript` — JavaScriptCore, no Node install needed). `indexedDB` is deliberately *not* mocked — see "File System Access API sync" below for which functions that rules out testing directly.

Run it with `./tests/run.sh` (optionally `./tests/run.sh someSubstring` to run only tests whose name contains that substring). No install step.

Add a test by dropping a `tests/cases/*.test.js` file with `test('description', function(){ ... })` calls. All app code plus every `tests/cases/*.test.js` file get concatenated and `eval()`'d *together* in one call, which is why a test can read/reassign `workstreams`, `items`, `categories`, `reviewCycles`, `mode`, `filterWorkstreamId` and the other top-level `let` state directly by bare name, and call any of the app's real functions — they're all sharing the same eval'd lexical scope. `tests/harness.js` calls `resetState()` (glue-defined: `seedDefaults()` + resets the UI-transient module vars + `normalizeData()`) and clears the fake DOM before every test, so tests never see state left over from another test — write fixtures at the top of each test rather than relying on run order. `seedDefaults()` itself creates zero workstreams (see "Data model" below); `resetState()`'s glue pushes one (`{name: 'Workstream 1', ...}`) back in immediately after, purely so the many existing tests that assume `workstreams[0]` already exists as a baseline convenience don't all need touching — the real product's fresh-install behavior is what `seedDefaults()` alone does.

Test files, by area: `workstreams.test.js`, `items.test.js`, `normalize.test.js`, `views.test.js` (status board rendering) predate categories/review/dashboard; `categories.test.js` (Admin CRUD, category-driven milestone seeding/reseeding), `review.test.js` (cycle start/confirm/complete/cancel, mid-cycle-add behavior, staleness), `dashboard.test.js` (rollup math, overdue/upcoming feeds), `modes.test.js` (mode switching, body visibility, tab active-state), `sync.test.js` (tombstones, merge, export/import, backups), `theme.test.js` (color scheme persistence/picker — see "Color scheme system" below), and `role.test.js` (the role ladder, picker/switcher, and per-role UI gating — see "Roles (RBAC)" below) cover everything added since.

One mock limitation worth knowing: the fake `<select>`/`<input>` elements don't parse `innerHTML` into `.value` the way a real browser does, so a test that wants to assert "this option got preselected" must check the rendered `innerHTML` string for `selected`/`value="..."` rather than reading `.value` after a `populate*Select()` call — see `tests/cases/items.test.js`'s "preselects the given workstream" test for the pattern. Tests that set form values *before* calling `save*()` set `.value` directly instead, which the mock does support.

## Architecture

The file has three logical sections in order: CSS styles, HTML structure, and a `<script>` block containing all application logic.

### Data model

All state is stored in `localStorage` under the key `"pulse-v1"` and serialised as JSON with this shape:

```json
{
  "programme": { "name": "...", "rolePasswords": { "reviewer": null, "editor": {"salt": "...", "hash": "..."}, "admin": null } },
  "workstreams": [...],
  "items": [...],
  "categories": [...],
  "reviewCycles": [...],
  "mode": "planning" | "review" | "dashboard" | "admin",
  "theme": "light" | "dark",
  "colorScheme": "standard" | "modern" | "dracula" | "github" | "githubdimmed", // see "Color scheme system" below
  "filterWorkstreamId": null | "wsId",
  "deletedWorkstreamIds": [{"id": "...", "deletedAt": 0}],
  "deletedItemIds": [{"id": "...", "deletedAt": 0}],
  "deletedMilestoneIds": [{"id": "...", "deletedAt": 0}],
  "deletedActionLogIds": [{"id": "...", "deletedAt": 0}],   // see "Merge and last-write-wins" below
  "deletedDecisionLogIds": [{"id": "...", "deletedAt": 0}]
}
```

`userRole` (`"visitor" | "editor" | "reviewer" | "admin"`, see "Roles (RBAC)" below) is deliberately **not** part of this blob — it lives under its own `localStorage` key entirely, and is never written to or read from a linked/synced data file at all.

A **workstream**:
```js
{ id: String, name: String, color: String, order: Number, updatedAt: Number,  // color is a name key from WS_COLORS
  actionLog: [{ id: String, text: String, owner: String, dueDate: String|null,
                completed: Boolean, completedAt: Number|null, cycleId: String, addedAt: Number,
                updatedAt: Number, flagged: Boolean }], // priority flag — see "Action Log" below
  decisionLog: [{ id: String, text: String, cycleId: String, addedAt: Number, updatedAt: Number, flagged: Boolean }] }
  // actionLog/decisionLog are the workstream-wide, review-cycle-spanning
  // action item/decision lists — see "Review mode" below. updatedAt on all
  // three (the workstream itself, and each actionLog/decisionLog entry)
  // drives field-level merging — see "Merge and last-write-wins" below.
```

An **item** — `itemType` distinguishes a plain scope item from a Journey (see "Journeys" below), but both share this one shape:
```js
{
  id: String,
  workstreamId: String|null, // null means "Unassigned" for a scope item (see "Quick-add scope items, the Unassigned bucket, and the Pending category") — always null for a Journey, which is never assigned to one at all (see "Journeys")
  categoryId: String|null,  // which Category's milestone template seeded this item — see "Categories (Admin mode)"; always null for a Journey (itemType 'journey' — see "Journeys"), which has no category at all
  name: String,
  status: 'not-started' | 'green' | 'amber' | 'red' | 'complete', // computed from milestones if any exist — see "Item status roll-up"
  startDate: String,       // 'YYYY-MM-DD'
  dueDate: String,         // 'YYYY-MM-DD'
  actualDate: String|null, // 'YYYY-MM-DD' — when it was actually finished; manual, never inferred from status
  updatedAt: Number,       // Date.now() — last-write-wins timestamp for merge (see "Data durability")
  milestones: [Milestone],
  itStatus: 'green' | 'amber' | 'red',       // IT readiness tag — see "IT/Business/Budget tags" below
  businessStatus: 'green' | 'amber' | 'red', // Business readiness tag
  budgetStatus: 'green' | 'amber' | 'red',   // Budget/funding status tag
  order: Number, // manual position within this item's workstream — see "Manual item order and drag-and-drop reordering"
  dependency: Boolean,           // is this item a dependency on an external party? — see "Dependencies" below (this field, its SPOC below, and the whole feature were originally named externalDelivery/externalDeliverySpoc/"External Delivery", renamed to match the newer Dependency category — an explicit user request)
  dependencySpoc: String|null,   // only ever non-null while dependency is true
  itemType: 'scope' | 'journey',  // see "Journeys" below — defaults to 'scope', backfilled by normalizeData()
  journeyId: String|null          // set on a scope item connected to a Journey (toggleJourneyConnection()) — see "Journeys"
}
```

**Workstreams now have an `updatedAt` — an explicit user request, following a broader question about what happens when several people run a review in parallel against the same synced file.** This reverses an earlier design choice (documented here for a long time as "Workstreams have no `updatedAt`, mirroring mytasks' projects") once that turned out to be a real, user-facing gap: without it, a workstream already known locally was skipped *entirely* on merge, no matter what changed on it elsewhere — including its `actionLog`/`decisionLog`, which is exactly what several reviewers running concurrent review cycles on the same workstream (via meeting minutes — see "Action Log" below) would be writing to at the same time. See "Merge and last-write-wins" below for the full mechanism this enables (`mergeWorkstreamFields()`) and for the one place a deleted workstream still has no `updatedAt`-based escape hatch (tombstone comparisons never did, and still don't).

**An item used to also carry its own `owner: String` — removed entirely (an explicit user request), not just hidden from the modal.** The field, its modal input, and the row's icon+name display are all gone; `saveItem()` no longer reads or writes it, and `normalizeData()` no longer backfills it. A pre-existing save that still has a leftover `owner` string on some items (from before this change) isn't actively stripped — it's just an inert, never-read property from here on, same "orphaned field is harmless" stance already taken elsewhere in this file (an orphaned review-cycle reference, an unused legacy `type` field, etc.) rather than adding a one-time migration pass for a value nothing displays or edits anymore. This is a distinct, unrelated concept from the Action Log's own Owner column (`actionLog[].owner`, above) — that one is a real action item's assignee and was untouched.

A **milestone** — nested inside its parent item, not a top-level array:
```js
{ id: String, name: String, dueDate: String|null, actualDate: String|null, status: 'not-started' | 'green' | 'amber' | 'red' | 'complete', notApplicable: Boolean }
// dueDate is only ever null while notApplicable is true (see "Not Applicable milestones" below) — an applicable milestone always has one.
```

**`actualDate` is deliberately manual, on both items and milestones** — it is never auto-filled when `status` is set to `'complete'`, and setting an `actualDate` never auto-sets `status` either. The two are independent: a PM might record the real finish date before formally marking something Complete (or vice versa, or never at all) — auto-linking them would take that judgment call away. `openItemModal()`/`saveItem()` read/write `#itemActualInput` the same way they do `startDate`/`dueDate`; `renderMilestonesEditor()` gives each milestone row its own actual-date input (`editingMilestones[idx].actualDate`) alongside its due-date input. Both default to `null` (not an empty string) when unset — `saveItem()` coerces the date input's `''` to `null` on save (`document.getElementById('itemActualInput').value || null`, and the same pattern per milestone), so "no actual date yet" is a single consistent falsy value rather than sometimes `''` and sometimes `null` depending on the code path.

Milestones don't have their own `owner`/`startDate` — they're a lightweight checklist against a single date, deliberately kept smaller than the item shape rather than reusing it. An item's own `status` **is** computed from its milestones whenever it has any — see "Item status roll-up" below.

A **category** (Admin mode's lookup table — see "Categories (Admin mode)" below):
```js
{ id: String, name: String, milestones: [String], order: Number, pending: Boolean, dependency: Boolean }
// milestones here are plain name strings, a template — not the {id,dueDate,status} shape an item's own milestones have.
// pending flags the one reserved category — see "Quick-add scope items..." below — default false.
// dependency flags the category that auto-checks a scope item's own dependency flag when
// picked — see "Dependencies" below — default false. Unlike pending, this carries no
// restrictions of its own (still deletable, still an ordinary pick everywhere).
// A Journey (see "Journeys" below) has no category at all — categoryId is null for one, not a
// reference to a reserved category the way an earlier version of this feature had.
```

A **review cycle** (see "Review mode" below):
```js
{
  id: String, workstreamId: String,
  startedAt: Number,              // Date.now()
  completedAt: Number | null,
  cancelledAt: Number | null, // legacy only — nothing sets this anymore; a cancelled cycle is deleted, not marked (see "Review mode")
  confirmations: [{ itemId: String, confirmed: Boolean, updatedAt: Number }],             // zero-milestone items only
  milestoneConfirmations: [{ milestoneId: String, confirmed: Boolean, updatedAt: Number }], // every milestone, across every item
  // A confirmation record is created once (on first toggle) and only ever
  // flipped afterward, never removed — see "Confirmation is milestone-level"
  // below and "Merge and last-write-wins" for why (the old shape,
  // {itemId|milestoneId, confirmedAt}, with presence-in-the-array meaning
  // confirmed, is what a pre-migration save still has on disk).
  itemCountAtClose: Number | undefined,      // snapshotted at completedAt — see snapshotReviewCycleCounts()
  confirmedCountAtClose: Number | undefined, // undefined on a still-active cycle, or one closed before this field existed
  minutes: Object | null,                    // {summary, decisions, nextSteps, actionItems, importedAt, updatedAt} — see "Review mode" below
  changeLog: [{                        // see logReviewChange()
    id: String, // stable id — see mergeReviewCycle()'s own union-by-id merge, "Merge and last-write-wins" below
    itemName: String, milestoneName: String | null, change: String, changedAt: Number,
    itemId: String, milestoneId: String | undefined, // stable ids, used only to re-sort for display — see orderedChangeLog()
    tagChange: { field: String, oldValue: String, newValue: String } | undefined,      // IT/Business/Budget
    statusChange: { oldValue: String, newValue: String } | undefined,                  // milestone status
    dateChange: { label: String, oldValue: String|null, newValue: String|null } | undefined // Due/Actual/Start
  }]
}
```
Review cycles are append-only history — nothing ever deletes one, `completedAt`/`cancelledAt` just get set once. Exactly one of `completedAt`/`cancelledAt` is non-null once a cycle is finished; both `null` means it's still active.

### Modes

`mode` (`'planning' | 'review' | 'dashboard' | 'admin'`, `MODES` array is the source of truth for the valid values) is the top-level navigation axis. Planning/Review/Dashboard are switched via the topbar's `.view-tabs` mode pill (`#tabPlanning`/`#tabDashboard`/`#tabReview`, in that left-to-right order — this pair has been swapped twice now by explicit user request: Dashboard originally led, then Planning/Dashboard swapped to put Dashboard first, then swapped back again to the current Planning-first order) and `setMode(m)`; Admin is deliberately **not** in that pill — it's its own plain `.btn` (`#tabAdmin`) sitting in the topbar's right-hand cluster, because it isn't workstream-scoped the way the other three are (see "The shared workstream selector" below) and grouping it with them would imply it was. Journeys used to be a 5th mode here (`mode === 'journeys'`), reached first from its own topbar `.btn` (for the same "never workstream-scoped" reason Admin still is one), then from its own dedicated sidebar row after a later, explicit user request — before a final, separate explicit user request folded it into Planning as a second sub-tab instead (`planningTab`, see "Journeys" further down for the full relocation history) — there is no `mode === 'journeys'` any more. External Deliveries used to be a 6th mode here too (`mode === 'external'`) — it went through its own multi-step relocation (sidebar row → `#topNavList` group → its own topbar `.btn`, `#tabExternal`) before a final, separate explicit user request removed the standalone mode/page concept entirely, folding it into a Planning-only quick-filter chip instead (see "Dependencies" above) — there is no `#tabExternal` and no `mode === 'external'` any more.

Planning, Review, and Dashboard share one container, `#scopedBody` (`.body-row` — sidebar + `#main`); Admin alone gets its own sidebar-less container (`#adminBody`, a plain `.main`, no sidebar), since categories aren't workstream-scoped. `render()` is the single top-level dispatcher: it shows `#scopedBody` for every mode except `admin` (which shows `#adminBody` instead), toggles `#reviewToolbar` (Review's own Scope Item Review/Action Log sub-tabs — see "Review mode" below) visible only for `mode === 'review'` and `#planningModeToolbar` (Planning's own Scope Items/Journeys sub-tabs — see "Journeys" below) visible only for `mode === 'planning'`, always calls `renderSidebar()`, and then calls exactly one of `renderMain()` (planning), `renderReview()`, `renderDashboard()`, `renderAdmin()` — each writes into its own container (`#main` for the first three, `#adminBody` for the last). `renderMain()` itself further dispatches on `planningTab`, between the status board (`renderStatusView()`) and the Journeys tree (`renderJourneys()`) — Journeys used to be a 5th mode dispatched from here directly, with its own dedicated sidebar nav row; it's Planning's own second sub-tab now (see "Journeys" below for that relocation's full history).

**Every mutation should call the top-level `render()`**, not a mode-specific render function, *unless* the call site is only ever reachable while that mode is already showing (e.g. `cycleMilestoneStatus()` calls the narrower `renderMain()`, safe because a milestone status badge only exists inside Planning's status board — nothing outside Planning can trigger it). `saveItem()`/`deleteItem()` in particular call the general `render()` because `openItemModal()` is reachable from both Planning (its own rows) and Review (a checklist row's status badge) — a narrower call would leave whichever mode *isn't* Planning showing stale content after a save made from Review mode.

### The shared workstream selector

`filterWorkstreamId` is no longer Planning-only — it's the single selector Planning (status board), Review (which workstream's cycle to show), and Dashboard (scopes the whole rollup) all read from, set from the one shared sidebar (`renderSidebar()`/`#wsList`) that's now visible in all three modes rather than duplicated per-mode. `null` means "All Workstreams" for Planning/Dashboard.

**Clicking a row in the sidebar — "All Workstreams" or a real workstream — always lands on Planning scoped to that selection, regardless of whatever mode was showing beforehand** — an explicit user request. This originally landed on Dashboard instead, per an earlier explicit user request; a later, separate explicit user request ("switching between workstream/all workstream should always go to planning tab") reversed the destination to Planning — only the target `mode` value changed, none of the surrounding mechanism. This is `selectWorkstreamFromSidebar(id)`, not the plain `setFilterWorkstream(id)` the sidebar used to call directly: it sets `filterWorkstreamId` and `mode = 'planning'` together in one `save()`/`render()`, the same combined-fields pattern `setUserRole()` already uses for its own, unrelated "jump to Dashboard, All Workstreams" case on leaving Admin mode (see "Roles (RBAC)" below — that one still deliberately targets Dashboard, since it fires for a completely different reason). `setFilterWorkstream()` itself stays deliberately mode-agnostic — every other caller depends on it *not* touching mode: `reviewDatesOverviewHtml()`'s own rows (see "Review mode" below) call it directly so picking a workstream from that staleness ranking lands on its Scope Item Review, not Planning; and the many places elsewhere in this file (and its test suite) that just want to narrow the scope without navigating away from the current mode.

**The sidebar's "All Workstreams" row is visually set apart from the real, per-workstream rows below it** (`.ws-row-all` — a user-reported readability request, since with the three sub-tab additions above it stopped being a plain fallback and became a real, frequently-used destination in its own right, not a row that should blend into the list): a thin `border-bottom` plus extra bottom padding separates it from the workstream rows, and it renders a neutral `fa-layer-group` icon (`.ws-row-all-icon`) in place of a real workstream's own colored `.ws-dot` — since it doesn't have (or stand in for) any single workstream's color, using one would misleadingly suggest it did.

**Review mode used to require a specific workstream unconditionally — a review cycle is one workstream at a time — but that stopped being true once every one of its sub-tabs grew an "All Workstreams" view of its own.** The Action Log has no cycle dependency at all (it's `workstream.actionLog`, tracked independently of any cycle's own lifecycle — see "Action Log" below), so a user request for an "All Workstreams" rollup of it (`allWorkstreamsActionLogHtml()`, see below) was the first crack in the old "Review always needs a workstream" gate — followed by the same request for Decision Log (`allWorkstreamsDecisionLogHtml()`), and finally by Scope Item Review itself gaining `reviewDatesOverviewHtml()` (see "Review mode" below) — a genuinely useful "All Workstreams" landing view in its own right, not just a fallback. With all three sub-tabs covered, **the gate is gone entirely, at all three of the layers it used to live at**:

1. **Entry**: `setMode('review')` never touches `reviewTab` at all any more — it used to force `reviewTab = 'actionLog'` whenever `filterWorkstreamId` was falsy (back when Scope Item Review had nothing to show without one); now every sub-tab has something to show, so whichever one was already selected just stays selected.
2. **Mid-session**: `setFilterWorkstream(id)` no longer hops `reviewTab` when the newly-picked `id` is `null` — it used to jump Scope Item Review over to Action Log for the same reason as above; picking "All Workstreams" while on Scope Item Review now just shows `reviewDatesOverviewHtml()` in place.
3. **Load-time backstop**: `normalizeData()` no longer falls back `mode` to `'planning'` for `mode: 'review'` with no `filterWorkstreamId` selected — that fallback existed only because `reviewTab: 'scope'` with no workstream used to be unrenderable; it isn't any more.

`renderReview()` itself reflects this: `!filterWorkstreamId` checks `reviewTab` and renders whichever "All Workstreams" view that tab has — `allWorkstreamsActionLogHtml()`, `allWorkstreamsDecisionLogHtml()`, or `reviewDatesOverviewHtml()` — there's no empty-state fallback left in this branch at all, since all three cases are now covered.

The topbar's Review tab (`#tabReview`) was already never marked disabled; `#tabReviewScope` (Review's own Scope Item Review sub-tab) no longer is either — the `.disabled`-toggle it used to inherit for the same "nothing to show without a workstream" reason, and the now-unused `.view-tab.disabled` CSS rule itself, were both removed once that stopped being true.

### RAG status

`STATUSES` (top of the `<script>` block) is the single source of truth for the six status values and their labels: Pending, Not Started, On Track (green), At Risk (amber), Off Track (red), Completed (blue — renamed from "Complete", an explicit user request). Status drives an item's color everywhere it appears — the status badge in the status board. Workstream color (`WS_COLORS`, a separate 8-hue palette) is used only for the sidebar dot and the status-board section header tint — it never colors an item, since status is the signal a programme dashboard is meant to surface at a glance.

**"Completed Late" is a computed DISPLAY label layered on top of the `'complete'` status — not a real 6th status value — an explicit user request.** A milestone (or a zero-milestone item) that's genuinely Complete but whose `actualDate` landed after its own `dueDate` renders "Completed Late" instead of "Completed" wherever its status badge shows; the underlying `status` field itself stays exactly `'complete'` everywhere else — `STATUS_SEVERITY`, the click-to-cycle in `cycleMilestoneStatus()` (still steps through the real 5 `STATUSES` values, "Completed Late" is never one of its stops), the item status roll-up, RAG count summaries, and the Excel export's own status vocabulary are all untouched. This was a deliberate scope choice, confirmed with the user up front, over the much larger alternative (a genuine 6th `STATUSES` entry touching severity ordering, the click-cycle, and RAG counts throughout the app) — mirrors the same "computed display override, data stays put" precedent Not Applicable milestones already set for their own frozen "N/A" badge (see "Not Applicable milestones" below).

`isCompletedLate(status, dueDate, actualDate, notApplicable)` is the one shared predicate: `status === 'complete' && !notApplicable && !!actualDate && !!dueDate && actualDate > dueDate && actualDate <= todayStr()` — deliberately the *exact* "late finish" condition `milestoneRowsHtml()`'s own `actualLate` flag already used for the red Actual-pill coloring (see "Delayed-milestone color coding" below), just additionally gated on the milestone actually being Complete (`actualLate` itself has no such gate — an open milestone with a late-looking Actual date still gets the red pill, but was never eligible for the "Completed Late" *label*, which only makes sense for something actually marked Complete). The `<= todayStr()` half carries over unchanged too: a completion date logged ahead of time, still in the future, isn't a late finish yet, so it reads as plain "Completed". `itemIsCompletedLate(it)` is the item-level counterpart — an item with milestones is "Completed Late" if *any* of its own applicable milestones themselves finished late (the same "one active problem always surfaces at the parent level" reasoning the RAG roll-up already applies, just for lateness instead of severity); a zero-milestone item falls back to its own manual `actualDate`/`dueDate` pair.

Applied everywhere a status badge/cell can show "Completed": the milestone row's own badge (`milestoneRowsHtml()`, reusing its existing `actualLate` local rather than recomputing it, combined with `m.status === 'complete'`), the item row's own badge (`itemRowHtml()` — for a Journey, rolled up from `connectedScopeItems(it.id).some(itemIsCompletedLate)` rather than the Journey's own, always-empty milestones/dates, mirroring how `computeJourneyStatus()` itself rolls up), the item modal's read-only computed Status badge (`updateItemStatusFieldMode()`, checked against the modal's own detached `editingMilestones` working copy — or, for a Journey, its connected items — not the item's possibly-stale pre-save data), and the Excel export's Scope Items sheet (both the item's own row and each milestone sub-row). **Deliberately not applied** to the modal's own editable milestone-status `<select>` (`renderMilestonesEditor()` — a real dropdown for choosing a real status value, "Completed Late" isn't one) or to a review cycle's historical change-log badges (`changeLogValueHtml()`'s `statusChange` old/new pair — those render a point-in-time snapshot of whatever status was actually set at that moment, and re-deriving "was this late" against data that may have changed since would misrepresent the historical record).

**"Completed Late" (15 characters) is longer than any of the real `STATUSES` labels, which broke the badge/column widths tuned for the old maximum ("Off Track"/"Not Started").** Two widths were bumped to fit it without wrapping or overflowing: `--item-grid-cols`' Status track (90px → 116px, both the shared default and the `.journeys-list` override) and `.milestone-status-cell`'s own internal first column (92px → 120px, the cell that packs the milestone row's Status badge and its Not Applicable toggle into the combined Status+Actions span — see "Column layout is a real CSS grid" above). The `white-space: nowrap` on `.milestone-status-cell > :first-child` is what made this one urgent rather than cosmetic: unlike the item row's own unconstrained badge (which would just wrap onto a second line if too narrow), a milestone row's badge was guaranteed to overflow the old 92px column and visually collide with the N/A toggle sitting right next to it. "Pending" (7 characters, see below) fits comfortably inside this same budget — it never needed a further widening of its own.

### Pending status

**A genuine 6th `STATUSES` entry — an explicit user request — unlike "Completed Late" just above, which was deliberately kept as a computed display override rather than a real status value.** `'pending'` means "no dates have been planned for this yet" — one step earlier in a milestone's lifecycle than `'not-started'` (which implies a real target date already exists, just hasn't been acted on). It's a real, hand-set/click-cycleable value like every other `STATUSES` entry: `cycleMilestoneStatus()`'s click-cycle, `#itemStatusSelect`'s options, `STATUS_SEVERITY`, RAG count summaries, the Dashboard's stacked bar/legend, and the Excel export's status vocabulary all pick it up for free by iterating `STATUSES`/`EXCEL_RAG_COLORS` generically — none of them needed a code change beyond the array/constant additions themselves.

**Naming collision, deliberate and unavoidable: this status shares its label with the unrelated, pre-existing *Pending category*** (see "Quick-add scope items, the Unassigned bucket, and the Pending category" below) — the reserved category a quick-added item starts in before real triage. The two are completely independent concepts that happen to reuse the same word for the same underlying reason ("nothing real has happened here yet") — the same tolerance this file already has for "Admin" (the role) vs. "Admin mode" (labeled "Settings" in the UI) or "Review" (the mode) vs. "Reviewer" (the role). In practice the two often *do* show up together on the same item without contradiction: a quick-added item sits in the Pending *category* with a status of Pending too, since its one milestone ("Scope Item Confirmed") is itself freshly seeded and un-dated — see below.

**Where it sits in the ordering.** `STATUSES` places `pending` first (`['pending', 'not-started', 'green', 'amber', 'red', 'complete']`) — the click-cycle now reads as a natural lifecycle: nothing planned yet → planned but not begun → on track/at risk/off track → done. `STATUS_SEVERITY` (lower = worse/weaker) places it just below `not-started` (`{ red: 0, amber: 1, green: 2, pending: 3, 'not-started': 4, complete: 5 }`) — a milestone with literally no date planned is judged a bigger gap than one that's dated but simply hasn't started, so an item mixing the two rolls up to `'pending'` as the more attention-worthy of the pair; both still rank far better than an active green/amber/red problem, the same "hasn't started needs no attention the way an active problem does" reasoning `'not-started'` already established.

**`milestonesForCategory(categoryId, dueDate)` now seeds every fresh milestone at `status: 'pending', dueDate: null`** — an explicit user request ("if a scope item gets initially milestones from a category, assign them status pending"), reversing the previous `status: 'not-started', dueDate: <the given date>` default. This is the single function every "get milestones from a category" call site already funneled through (a brand-new item's initial seed, switching category while still creating one, an existing item's destructive category switch, `applyScopeCategory()`'s Pending-category-to-real-category swap — see "Switching an existing item's category" and "Quick-add scope items..." below), so changing it there is what makes the new default apply everywhere uniformly, with no per-call-site special-casing. The `dueDate` parameter itself is kept only for call-site/signature stability — several callers still pass the item's own due date, but it's no longer read inside the function at all, since seeding a real-looking date would contradict a status that specifically means "no date planned yet". **Deliberately not extended to `applyCategoryMilestoneSync()`'s own incremental "template gained a new milestone, retroactively add it to items already using this category" path** (see "Category template edits sync to items already using that category" below) — that newly-appended milestone still gets `status: 'not-started', dueDate: it.dueDate`, unchanged. This is a narrower reading of the request than a global "every code path that ever creates a milestone" sweep: an item already mid-execution picking up one new template step shouldn't have that single row read as "nothing planned at all" the way a genuinely fresh item's *entire* checklist should.

**A milestone's own Due date field now uses the exact same "+" ghost/reveal pattern Actual date already established** (see "Every inline date field renders as a 'pill'" and the Actual-date-ghost paragraph above) — an explicit user request ("the date field should act like the actual date field... should only show if I hit the +"). `revealedDueIds`/`revealDueDate(milestoneId)`/`cancelRevealDueDate(milestoneId)` are `revealedActualIds`/`revealActualDate()`/`cancelRevealActualDate()`'s exact counterparts (same UI-only, not-persisted `Set`, same focus-on-reveal, same Escape-while-still-empty cancel-back-to-ghost behavior), and `.due-date-ghost` shares `.actual-date-ghost`'s CSS rules outright (`.actual-date-ghost, .due-date-ghost { ... }`) rather than being a copy-pasted duplicate block. **The ghost keys off a genuinely missing `dueDate`, not off the status being `'pending'` specifically** — mirroring how Actual's own ghost has never checked status either. In practice the two coincide by construction (a fresh `'pending'` milestone is exactly what starts with `dueDate: null`), but they're independent checks: a `'pending'` milestone that already has a real due date (hand-set, imported, or revealed-and-filled) shows the normal editable pill, not the ghost, regardless of its status; and the pending-ness of the status doesn't, by itself, force the ghost to reappear once a date exists. During a review (`reviewCycle` passed to `milestoneRowsHtml()`), Due is still the read-only computed span it always was — now rendering a plain `"—"` instead of a garbled `fmtDate(null)` when there's genuinely nothing to show yet.

**Two existing "always backfill a missing due date from the parent item" guards needed the same exemption `notApplicable` milestones already carved out for themselves** — otherwise a deliberately-dateless `'pending'` milestone would silently pick up a real-looking date on the very next save or load, defeating the point: `normalizeData()`'s `if (!m.dueDate && !m.notApplicable) m.dueDate = it.dueDate;` backfill is now `&& m.status !== 'pending'`, and `saveItem()`'s milestone-mapping fallback (`dueDate: m.notApplicable ? (m.dueDate || null) : (m.dueDate || manualDueDate)`) now checks `(m.notApplicable || m.status === 'pending')` on the same left-hand branch. Both are narrow, additive exemptions — an *applicable*, *non-pending* milestone still always ends up with a real due date exactly as before.

**As soon as a `'pending'` milestone actually receives a due date, it auto-advances to `'not-started'`** — an explicit user request ("as soon as one milestone in a scope item has received a date, switch status from Pending to Not Started"). Implemented in both places a due date can actually get set: `updateMilestoneDateField()` (the status board's own inline pill/ghost-reveal edit) and `saveItem()`'s milestone-mapping (the item modal's plain, always-visible input — see just above for why that one has no ghost of its own but still needs the same rule, since a user can just as easily type a date directly into it). Both check the *old* status was `'pending'` and the *new* value is truthy before flipping — deliberately scoped to the `dueDate` field only, not `actualDate`: receiving an actual-completion date doesn't imply this milestone was ever actually planned, so setting one first (an edge case, but reachable via hand-edited/imported data) must not trigger the advance. It's also one-directional — nothing reverts a milestone *back* to `'pending'` once it's left, the same "advance, never un-advance" convention every other automatic status nudge in this app already follows (Not Applicable preserves rather than resets `status`; nothing un-completes a milestone either). `updateMilestoneDateField()` recomputes the item's own rolled-up `status` immediately after (a plain date edit otherwise never touches it, since dates don't normally factor into `computedStatusFromMilestones()` — this is the one case they indirectly do, through the status change the date triggered) and logs the transition as its own `statusChange` change-log entry, right alongside the `dateChange` entry the date edit itself already logs — so a reviewer reading history later sees two real entries ("Due: … → …" and "Status changed to Not Started"), not one date edit silently implying a status change nobody can see happened.

**The Actual-date ghost itself is disabled (rendered greyed-out and non-clickable, not omitted) whenever the milestone has no due date yet** — a related, explicit user request ("adding an actual date should only work if a due date is available"). `actualGhostOpenable` (`actualEditable && !!m.dueDate`) gates only the *open* affordance: a milestone with no due date still shows the same `.actual-date-ghost` button (same "always show a real, visible control, just inert" convention already used for the Not Applicable toggle and the review confirm toggle elsewhere in this file) but with `disabled` set and no `onclick`, plus a "Set a due date first" tooltip in place of the normal one. `.actual-date-ghost:disabled`/`:disabled:hover` override the plain `:hover` rule's brightening (one extra pseudo-class of specificity, so a disabled ghost never looks interactive on mouseover). This only gates *opening* a not-yet-started Actual entry — an Actual date that's already set (however it got there: hand-edited data, an older import, or set back when this restriction didn't exist) still renders as the normal, fully editable pill regardless of whether a due date exists now; the rule is about starting something new, not retroactively locking existing data. The moment a due date is actually set (revealed-and-filled, or via the auto-advance path just above triggering a re-render), the ghost becomes clickable on the very next render, since `actualGhostOpenable` is recomputed fresh every time — no separate wiring needed.

**The disabled ghost also swaps its icon from the plain "+" to a lock (`fa-lock`) — a user-reported first-time-user readability gap.** Due and Actual's own ghosts sit in adjacent columns and, at rest, look near-identical (same 20px circle, same faint color) — the *only* thing distinguishing a disabled Actual ghost from the enabled Due ghost right next to it used to be a subtler opacity difference (`.3` vs `.55`), easy to miss at a glance, especially for someone who hasn't learned yet that one of the two can even be disabled. `milestoneRowsHtml()`'s disabled branch now renders `<i class="fa-solid fa-lock">` instead of the shared `fa-solid fa-plus` every other (enabled) ghost in this app uses — a shape difference that registers even where the opacity difference alone doesn't. The opacity difference itself is untouched, still layered underneath as a secondary cue, not replaced by the icon swap.

**The item modal's own milestone editor (`renderMilestonesEditor()`) does *not* get a matching ghost/reveal treatment** — that editor has never used the ghost pattern for either date field (Actual included, which is a plain always-visible `<input type="date">` there, unlike the status board's own pill-plus-ghost), so a fresh `'pending'` milestone with `dueDate: null` there just renders as an ordinary, momentarily-blank date input — the same way any other blank field in that flat form already looks. `esc(m.dueDate)` already coerces `null` to `''` safely (it did before this feature too), so this needed no code change beyond a clarifying comment. If a ghost/reveal treatment is ever wanted inside the modal too, that would be a deliberate, separate follow-up, not an oversight here. The modal's Actual input is likewise never disabled based on Due, unlike the board's own ghost — that restriction is specific to the ghost/reveal *affordance*, not a hard rule on the underlying data, and the modal has no such affordance to gate in the first place.

**Color**: `--stat-pending`/`--stat-pending-bg` were added to `:root`'s Standard palette and to all four other color schemes' light *and* dark blocks (10 declarations total — see "Color scheme system" below) — a status that only renders correctly in one of five schemes would be a real regression for anyone using the others. Standard's own pair (`#7a6fd1`/`#ece9fb`, a muted indigo/violet — visually distinct from every other `--stat-*`/`--ws-*` hue already in use) is also what `EXCEL_RAG_COLORS.pending` mirrors (`FF7A6FD1`/`FFECE9FB`), matching that constant's own established "always the Standard scheme's values, regardless of the active scheme" convention (see "Export to Excel" above). Every other scheme's `--stat-pending` reuses an existing, real token already defined *within that same scheme* (its own purple- or pink-family workstream color) rather than inventing a wholly new hex a fourth or fifth time — e.g. GitHub/GitHub Dimmed both reuse their own `--ws-purple` (Primer's real "merged" purple), Dracula reuses its own real `--ws-pink`, Modern reuses its own real `--ws-purple`.

### Rendering pattern

Direct DOM re-renders (no virtual DOM or framework). `render()` does a full re-render (topbar bits + sidebar + main); `renderSidebar()` and `renderMain()` handle their halves independently. Every state mutation calls `save()` then one of these. `renderMain()` always renders the status board (`renderStatusView()`) — if you add a mutation that should be reflected live, make sure it calls `render()` (or at least the render function covering whatever it changed), not a narrower one that happens to work for today's call site.

### Status board (`renderStatusView`)

One `.ws-section` per visible workstream (filtered by `filterWorkstreamId` via `visibleWorkstreams()`), each showing a RAG count summary (`STATUSES` filtered to non-zero counts) and its items sorted by `order` (see "Manual item order and drag-and-drop reordering" below — this used to be `dueDate`, but a reviewer/planner reordering the list by hand needs that order to actually stick, not get re-sorted out from under them the moment a due date changes). Review's own `renderReview()` sorts its item list the exact same way, for the same reason: it's meant to be *the same list* as Planning's, not an independently-sorted one. `itemRowHtml(it)` renders the item's own row plus, if `expandedItemIds` (a module-level `Set`, UI-only — not persisted, resets on reload) has that item's id, a small `milestoneHeaderHtml()` column-label row and its nested milestone rows (`milestoneRowsHtml`, in plain array order — see "Category template edits sync to items already using that category" below for why this is *not* sorted by `dueDate`) immediately after. Clicking a milestone's own status badge calls `cycleMilestoneStatus(itemId, milestoneId)`, which steps through `STATUSES` in array order and wraps — a quicker way to update a milestone's RAG than opening the item modal (and, per "Item status roll-up" below, recomputes the parent item's status too).

**"Expand all"/"Collapse all" per section** (`expandAllToggleHtml()`, `toggleExpandAllForItems(ids)`) — an explicit user request: once a section holds more than a handful of multi-milestone items, expanding each one individually via its own chevron gets tedious. `expandAllToggleHtml(sectionItems)` is called from both `renderStatusView()`'s per-workstream `.ws-section-header` (passed that workstream's own `displayItems` — already filtered by search/status/external, and already covering both the main list and its External Deliveries sub-section, since `displayItems` is the pre-split array both draw from) and `unassignedSectionHtml()`'s own header (passed `uItems`) — one independent toggle per section, not a single global control. It filters down to just the items actually worth expanding (`it.milestones.length > 0` — a zero-milestone item's chevron is inert, see `itemRowHtml()`'s own `expandable` check) and renders nothing at all (not a disabled button) if none qualify. **Icon-only, not a labeled button** — a `fa-angles-down`/`fa-angles-up` double-chevron reusing `.row-icon-btn`'s own small-icon-button styling (the same class the row's own drag/edit/delete icons already use) rather than `.btn`, per an explicit user request matching a reference screenshot — and **positioned at the very start of the header, right before the workstream dot/name**, not trailing after the RAG pills where it first landed. Both the icon direction and its `title` tooltip reflect the group's *aggregate* state, the same pattern `historyShowAllWs`'s own "Show more"/"Show less" toggle already uses: pointing down, "Expand all", as long as at least one qualifying item in the group isn't currently expanded; pointing up, "Collapse all", only once every one of them already is. Clicking it calls `toggleExpandAllForItems(ids)` with that exact, already-filtered id list baked directly into the `onclick` attribute (`[${ids.map(id => \`'${id}'\`).join(',')}]`) rather than re-deriving the group from `workstreamId` at click time — deliberately, so a click only ever acts on what's actually visible on screen at that moment (never an item a filter is currently narrowing out, and never accidentally including a Journey, which also has `workstreamId: null` like an Unassigned item but is filtered out of `uItems` well before this point). `toggleExpandAllForItems()` calls the general `render()` (matching `toggleItemExpanded()`'s own choice, for the same reason — see below) rather than a narrower mode-specific render function, since a later, explicit user request extended it to the Journeys sub-tab too (see "Journeys" below) — `render()` is what re-dispatches to `renderMain()`, which is in turn what picks between Planning's status board and the Journeys tree based on `planningTab`. Neither function is role-gated — expanding/collapsing is non-destructive UI state, the same unrestricted-at-every-role treatment the per-item chevron already has.

**Planning's own scope-item search box** (`#planningSearchInput`, `setPlanningSearch()`/`clearPlanningSearch()`/`matchesPlanningSearch()`) — the first of a set of proactively-suggested UI improvements, picked as the highest-value/lowest-effort one: with `filterWorkstreamId` the only other narrowing tool, finding one item by name in a workstream with many items meant scanning the list by eye. It's a plain, case-insensitive substring match against `it.name` only (deliberately not milestone names too — a quick "find one scope item" tool, not a general-purpose query). The input itself lives in a new static toolbar, `#planningSearchToolbar` (shown only for `mode === 'planning' && planningTab === 'scope'`, toggled in `render()` the same way `#reviewToolbar` already is for Review — hidden on the Journeys sub-tab too, see "Journeys" below, since a flat item search has nothing to filter there) — sitting *outside* `#main`, not inside it, specifically so `setPlanningSearch()` can call the narrower `renderMain()` on every keystroke without that re-render ever stealing focus from the input itself (the same reasoning behind keeping `editingMilestones`' own row edits from re-rendering on every keystroke — see "Milestone checklist" above). `planningSearchQuery` (module-level, UI-only, not persisted, lowercased/trimmed once on input) is read by both `renderStatusView()` and `unassignedSectionHtml()`:
- A workstream with zero matches while a query is active is skipped entirely — no header, no "No items yet." placeholder — rather than cluttering results with an empty section; the RAG pill counts in a workstream's header that *does* still show are always computed from its real, unfiltered item list regardless (matching the same "the split narrows what's listed, never what's counted" precedent the Dependency sub-section above already set), and the Dependency split itself still applies on top of the filtered results (a matching item still lands in its correct main-list-vs-dependency-sub-section slot).
- The Unassigned section is filtered the same way, and — while actively searching — omitted entirely even at Editor+ if it has no matches: the "+ Add item" quick-add prompt that would otherwise keep an empty Unassigned section visible for an Editor isn't useful while someone's trying to find an existing item, not add a new one.
- Review, Dashboard, and Journeys are completely untouched by this — `planningSearchQuery` is never read outside `renderStatusView()`/`unassignedSectionHtml()`, so switching mode away from Planning leaves the search term remembered (not persisted across reloads, but not reset on a mode switch either) with zero effect on what any other mode shows.

**Quick-filter status chips** (`#planningStatusFilterChips`, `renderPlanningStatusChips()`/`setPlanningStatusFilter()`/`matchesPlanningStatusFilter()`) — a later, explicit user request, and a deliberately lighter alternative to a full saved-filter-presets feature that was discussed but not built: one toggle chip per `STATUSES` entry, sitting directly below the search box in the same static `#planningSearchToolbar`, colored with that status's own `--stat-*`/`--stat-*-bg` pair via inline `--chip-color`/`--chip-bg` custom properties (a per-status CSS class isn't possible here, since the status set itself is data in `STATUSES`, not something the stylesheet knows about). **Multi-select** — clicking a chip toggles its membership in `planningStatusFilters` (a `Set` of `STATUSES` ids, empty = no filter); any number of chips can be active at once, and an item matching *any* selected chip passes. This was originally single-select (one active chip at a time, clicking it again cleared it) — a later, separate explicit user request ("make search pills multi select") changed it to the current shape; `setPlanningStatusFilter(statusId)` now adds/removes `statusId` from the set instead of assigning/nulling a single value. `matchesPlanningStatusFilter(it)` is `matchesPlanningSearch()`'s exact counterpart, kept as its own separate predicate rather than folded together — both call sites (`renderStatusView()`, `unassignedSectionHtml()`) AND the two together (`it => matchesPlanningSearch(it) && matchesPlanningStatusFilter(it))`), so search and status filter compose rather than override each other. **It checks more than just the item's own status** — an explicit user request ("the search filter should also filter Milestones with the respective status"): `planningStatusFilters.has(it.status)` is checked first, but if that doesn't match, it falls back to `(it.milestones || []).some(m => !m.notApplicable && planningStatusFilters.has(m.status))`. This matters because an item's own status is only ever the *weakest* link across its milestones (see "Item status roll-up" above) — a plain item-status check alone would mean a chip like "Completed" almost never surfaces anything, since it would require *every* milestone to be complete simultaneously; matching on individual milestones too means an item shows up the moment *any* one of its milestones is in a selected state, even while its own rolled-up status still reads worse. A `notApplicable` milestone is excluded from this check, the same "never counts toward or against anything" treatment it gets everywhere else in this app. Every "narrows the list, never the counts" and "omit an empty workstream/Unassigned section entirely" rule the search box already established (see the three bullets above) applies identically here, just gated on `(planningSearchQuery || planningStatusFilters.size)` instead of `planningSearchQuery` alone. `renderPlanningStatusChips()` is called unconditionally from the top of `render()` (alongside `renderSidebar()`) rather than only when `mode === 'planning'` — cheap enough (a handful of small buttons) that threading a mode check through here wasn't worth it, and it means the chips' active state is never stale by the time Planning's toolbar becomes visible again. Like `planningSearchQuery`, `planningStatusFilters` is module-level, UI-only, not persisted, and not reset on a mode switch — only on reload. The same function also appends a 7th chip after these 6 — a standalone Dependencies on/off toggle, separated by a small `.chip-divider` — see "Dependencies" below for that one's own fuller history (it used to be a whole separate mode/page before landing here). **Journeys, Action Log, and Decision Log each later grew their own quick-filter chip row too, the same explicit user request that added their search boxes** ("add search pills to the newly added searches") — see "Journeys" and "Action Log" below for each one's own shape (Journeys reuses the identical multi-select `STATUSES` chip row against a Journey's own computed status; Action Log/Decision Log have no RAG status field, so theirs are built from different underlying booleans instead).

**Plan-date ranges always show the year** (`fmtDateY`, distinct from the plain `fmtDate` used for other, single-date, same-year-implied contexts like backup timestamps and review-cycle start dates) — a Start/Due range legitimately spans years (e.g. a scope item starting one calendar year and due the next), and a bare "Jul 26 → Jan 1" reads as backwards or same-year when it isn't. Used for the item row's computed range, the item modal's computed-range badge, and the review-mode item row — anywhere a Start→Due *range* is rendered as text.

**There is deliberately no page-wide column header.** An earlier version added one sticky `.item-row-header` above all `.ws-section`s, labeling all the shared columns once for the whole page — this got removed because it implied those labels applied uniformly to milestone rows too, when a milestone only ever populates a handful of them (Milestone/Due/Actual/Status). `milestoneHeaderHtml()` instead renders a small `.milestone-header` scoped to *one* expanded item, directly above that item's own milestone rows — appearing and disappearing with the same chevron toggle that shows/hides the milestones themselves, so the labels are only ever visible attached to the specific list they describe.

**Column layout is a real CSS grid, not flexbox** — `.item-row`, `.milestone-header`, and `.milestone-sub-row` all share one `grid-template-columns` value, `--item-grid-cols` (a `:root` custom property, currently 11 columns: Chevron, Name, an always-blank column (formerly Owner — see below), Milestones-badge, an always-blank spacer (see below), Plan-dates, a second always-blank spacer, Milestone-Due, Tags/Milestone-Actual (item-only Tags on the item's own row, the milestone's own Actual-date input on a milestone row), Status, Actions), so a milestone's Due/Actual/Status line up directly under its parent item's columns and `milestoneHeaderHtml()`'s labels line up over both. **Owner was removed as a concept entirely (an explicit user request) — the modal field, the row's icon+name display, and the `owner` property on the item's own data shape are all gone — but its grid column stays, now permanently empty (`<span></span>`), rather than collapsing `--item-grid-cols` further and renumbering every explicit `grid-column:N` reference that comes after it (milestone name's own span, the review confirm toggle's `grid-column`, etc.).** That renumbering would touch several already-tuned, comment-documented spots across `itemRowHtml()`/`milestoneRowsHtml()`/`milestoneHeaderHtml()`/Review markup for a request that was really about the *field*, not the layout geometry — an empty placeholder cell is the same safe, established pattern already used everywhere else in this grid for a column a given row has nothing to put in (see below). The `.item-owner` CSS class itself is untouched, since Action Log's own, unrelated Owner column (a real, still-present concept for action items) still uses it. **A later user request moved the Milestones-count badge from column 3 to column 4, swapping widths with the blank former-Owner column next to it** — the badge only needs 90px, so the extra width is better spent letting `.item-name` itself span columns 2-3 (`grid-column: 2 / 4`, set inline in `itemRowHtml()`) instead of sitting in column 2 alone. A separate, later request removed the grid's old "Actual" placeholder column outright (it used to sit right after Plan-dates, reserved for the item's own `actualDate` field even though nothing was ever rendered there — that field is only ever edited in the item modal) rather than leaving it shrunk-but-present the way the Owner column was kept — every explicit `grid-column:N` reference from that point on (the review confirm toggle, the Status/N/A spanning cell) shifted down by one accordingly. **A still later, separate explicit user request ("add a 20px spacer column between 4 and 5") inserted a brand-new, purely decorative blank column right after the Milestones-badge, pushing Plan-dates and everything after it (the second blank spacer, Milestone-Due, Tags/Milestone-Actual, Status, Actions, and the Review confirm toggle's own implicit trailing column) up by one again** — `itemRowHtml()` needed one new bare `<span></span>` inserted right before its `planDatesHtml` cell (its only auto-placed cell that would otherwise have silently slid into the new column instead of Plan-dates), `milestoneRowsHtml()`'s own name span widened from `grid-column: 2 / 6` to `2 / 7` to keep swallowing every item-only column up through the new one, `milestoneHeaderHtml()` gained one more blank `<span>` in its own auto-placed sequence to match, and every remaining explicit `grid-column:N`/`N/M` reference in this file (`.milestone-status-cell`'s own `9/11` → `10/12`, the Review confirm toggle's own `11` → `12`) shifted by exactly one, the same mechanical renumbering the two earlier column changes above already went through. **This means every cell in every row must be a real element, even when empty** — an omitted element (the old code used to render the milestone-count badge as a plain empty string `''` when an item had zero milestones) collapses that grid column entirely for that row, shifting every subsequent cell left and breaking alignment with rows that do have content there. `itemRowHtml()`'s `milestoneBadge` and `milestoneRowsHtml()`'s several deliberately-empty `<span></span>` placeholders (for the columns a milestone row has nothing to put in) exist specifically to hold their column open — `tests/cases/views.test.js`'s "still renders the milestone-count-badge element (empty)" test guards this exact regression. `milestoneRowsHtml()` also reuses the `.item-chevron` class (holding a small diamond icon instead of a chevron) for its first cell, and explicitly spans its Name cell across the Name/blank/Milestones/blank/Plan-dates columns (`grid-column: 2 / 7`) since a milestone only ever has a name to put in that whole span and a single column's width was truncating longer milestone names. The one exception to "every cell must be a real element" is the "No items yet" placeholder row, which overrides `grid-template-columns:1fr` inline to span the full width as a single cell rather than trying to fill every column with one string.

**There is no Notes field** — items used to have a free-text `notes` field shown in its own row column, removed entirely (data model, item modal, row column) since it wasn't earning its column's width. If it comes back, it should be a deliberate re-add, not a resurrected dead column.

**`.ws-section` never clips its own content (`overflow: hidden` was removed from it)** — the rounded-card look comes from rounding `.ws-section-header`'s top corners and `.ws-section-add`'s bottom corners directly, not from clipping the section box. A row wider than the section (more columns than fit at the current window width) stays reachable via `.main`'s own `overflow: auto` instead of being silently cut off — this was a real bug once: the Due/Actual columns added for milestones pushed Status/Actions past the section's clipped edge with no way to reach them. One of the 10 columns (the always-blank spacer) is deliberately kept narrow rather than removed outright, specifically to keep the row's total width comfortably under typical window widths so this scroll fallback rarely has to engage in practice — a second such column, an "Actual" placeholder that used to sit right after Plan-dates, was later removed entirely rather than just kept narrow, per an explicit user request ("remove column 6" — see "Column layout is a real CSS grid" above).

**`.item-row`/`.milestone-header`/`.milestone-sub-row` all use asymmetric padding — `20px` on the right, `14px` on the left** (`padding: 9px 20px 9px 14px` / `5px 20px 5px 14px` / `7px 20px 7px 14px`) — a user-reported fix: the trailing Actions column (and, in Review, the confirm/complete toggle sitting beyond it at `grid-column:11`) read as uncomfortably flush against the card's own right edge with the original symmetric `14px`. All three share the wider right value specifically because they share `--item-grid-cols` — changing just `.item-row` would have left milestone rows sitting a visible few pixels further right than their parent item's own row, breaking the "everything lines up" invariant the whole shared-grid design depends on (see "Column layout is a real CSS grid" above). Action Log inherits the same padding via its shared `.item-row` class, but its own trailing columns are a fully bespoke grid unrelated to `--item-grid-cols` — see "Action Log" below.

**Dates are editable directly on the row, not only via the item modal — with one deliberate exception.** An item's own Plan dates (`.item-dates-inline` — Start/Due as two date fields with a `→` between them, shown only when it has zero milestones — see "Item plan date range roll-up" below) and each milestone sub-row's Due/Actual dates all render through `dateInputHtml(value, attrs)` rather than formatted text. Their `onblur` (not `onchange`) calls `updateItemDateField(itemId, field, value)` / `updateMilestoneDateField(itemId, milestoneId, field, value)`, which write straight into the matching `items`/`milestones` entry and `save(); render()` — no modal round-trip needed for a quick date correction. `onblur` specifically, not `onchange`: Chrome fires `change`/`input` on a date input for every partial keystroke while a segment is still incomplete (`this.value` reads `''` until all three segments are filled), so `onchange` would save/re-render (destroying the focused input) after a single digit — `onblur` only commits once the user actually leaves the field. Both update functions additionally no-op (no save, no render, no history log entry) when the new value equals the field's current value, so a bare focus+blur that never actually changed anything — including revealing then abandoning an empty Actual ghost, see below — doesn't get recorded as a change. Neither update function touches `status` — date edits are orthogonal to the milestone-status roll-up. Start/Due don't accept an empty value (`onblur="if(this.value) ..."` guards the call) since a blank plan date isn't meaningful; Actual does accept empty (`this.value || null`) since clearing a previously-set actual date is a real, intentional action.

**The Start/Due `if(this.value)` guard used to leave an incomplete edit completely silent — a user-reported bug that read as spontaneous, unexplained data loss.** If a segment was left unfinished when the input blurred (`this.value` still `''`), the guard correctly skipped the write — but did nothing else either, so the input kept *displaying* whatever partial text had been typed, which looks exactly like a successfully saved edit. The real, unchanged stored date only resurfaced on the next unrelated `render()` (switching to Dashboard and back, cycling a milestone's status, anything that redraws the row from `items` again) — by then looking like the date had silently "reset itself" with no visible cause, rather than what actually happened: the edit never committed in the first place. Fixed by adding an `else this.value = '<the real stored date>'` alongside the existing guard on all three call sites (item Start, item Due, milestone Due — Actual is unaffected, since it already always commits via `this.value || null` rather than guarding on emptiness) — an incomplete edit now snaps the input back to the true value immediately on blur, visibly rejecting it right away instead of silently discarding it and letting a later, unrelated render "reveal" the loss.

**A second, separate bug shared the exact same "silent revert" symptom but a completely different cause, only found by reviewing a screen recording of it happening: picking a date from the native calendar popup (`showPicker()`, opened via the pill's own calendar icon) doesn't reliably fire this input's `blur` afterward.** The popup updates the input's own displayed value immediately (confirmed on video: the pill visibly showed the newly-picked date right away), but since `onblur` was the *only* handler that ever wrote a date into `items`/`milestones`, and it never ran, the edit was never actually committed — the display just happened to already show the right value, coincidentally, as an artifact of the native input's own internal state, completely independent of the app's data. This looked identical to the first bug (the edit "worked," then silently reverted on the next unrelated render — a mode switch, in the reported case) but survived the first fix untouched, since the value was never incomplete — it was complete and valid the whole time, just never delivered to the app. Confirmed directly: dispatching `input`+`change` on a date field's real DOM node with a complete value, *without ever dispatching `blur`*, left `items`/`milestones` completely unchanged — proving `onblur` alone isn't a reliable commit point regardless of the value's completeness. Fixed by adding `onchange="if(this.value) update...(...)"` (the same guarded call as `onblur`, minus the reset-on-incomplete `else`) to every date input in the app — item Start, item Due, milestone Due, and milestone Actual (the one field that didn't need the first fix still needed this one, since it has exactly the same picker-doesn't-blur exposure). `change` fires the moment the popup commits a value, regardless of whatever focus/blur quirk that popup has; `onblur` stays alongside it — it's still what a completed keyboard edit relies on when the user tabs/clicks away, and what resets an incomplete one back per the first fix above. Both functions already no-op when the incoming value equals the field's current one, so `onchange` and a later `onblur` both firing for the same edit is harmless, not a double-commit.

**A third, separate bug — user-reported as "commits after i hit one char" — shared the same input/onchange machinery the second fix added, but only surfaced on an *already-populated* field.** Chrome fires `change` on a date input for every keystroke that produces some syntactically complete-looking value, not just once at the very end once every segment has genuinely been filled in by the user. For a blank field that's harmless (`this.value` reads `''` until all three segments are filled — the existing `if(this.value)` guard covers exactly that case). But for a field that already has a stored date, completing just the *first* segment the user touches — e.g. typing one digit of a new month while day/year are still whatever they were before — already reads as a complete, valid date to Chrome: just one built from the newly-typed segment plus the *stale, not-yet-edited* sibling segments. The `if(this.value)` guard can't tell that apart from a value the user actually finished typing, so it passed, `updateItemDateField()`/`updateMilestoneDateField()` committed that wrong composite value immediately, and the `render()` call inside them rebuilt the row — destroying the still-focused, still-mid-edit input and silently swallowing every further keystroke. Confirmed directly: typing a genuinely different month into an already-dated field and watching `document.activeElement` — focus was lost, and the final stored value reflected only the first one or two digits typed, never the day/year the user typed afterward, since those keystrokes had nowhere left to land.

**First fixed with a debounce (`debounceDateChange(key, applyFn)`) — since replaced outright, not layered on, once a follow-up user report ("still has problems with 4 digit years... commits after 2 digits") showed the debounce didn't actually close the gap.** The debounce coalesced a burst of same-field `change` events into a single commit some fixed delay after the *last* one, on the theory that no render happens mid-burst so the input is never destroyed mid-edit. That's true for *fast* consecutive keystrokes, but testing it digit-by-digit against the YEAR segment specifically (not month/day) found the real, deeper problem: unlike month/day, which need both digits typed before Chrome's `.value` stops reading `''`, the year segment reports a genuine, non-empty, zero-padded value after just the *first* digit (confirmed directly — typing a single "2" into an already-dated field's year segment produced a complete-looking ISO value immediately, from that one digit alone). No fixed debounce window is safe against that: any pause between year digits at all — ordinary typing cadence, not an edge case — still let a wrong, truncated year commit and destroy the input, exactly like the original bug. There's no reliable browser signal for "every segment is genuinely done" short of the field actually losing focus, so no amount of tuning the delay fixes this category of bug; a fundamentally different mechanism was needed.

**The actual fix removes `onchange`-driven auto-commit for keyboard edits entirely.** A keyboard edit now commits *only* on `onblur` — a real, one-time, deterministic "the user is done here" signal that doesn't try to infer completeness from the value's shape at all, so it's completely immune to Chrome's per-segment quirks (year needing 1 digit, month/day needing 2, or any future surprise in that vein). That leaves the *other* documented reason `onchange` existed at all — the native picker not reliably firing `blur` — needing its own, separate, precise handling rather than a heuristic: `armPickerCommit(inputEl, applyFn)` arms a one-time `change` listener at the *exact moment* the calendar icon's own `onclick` calls `showPicker()` — since that icon (with the input's own native picker indicator hidden) is the *only* way this app's UI ever opens the picker, the one `change` event this listener can ever catch is a genuine picker selection, never an ordinary keystroke, which never goes anywhere near this code path at all. The listener removes itself after firing once (`{once:true}`, plus an explicit `removeEventListener` inside its own handler as a defensive backup) so an abandoned picker (opened, then dismissed with Escape, nothing selected) leaves nothing armed that could misfire against a later, unrelated keyboard edit. `dateInputHtml(value, attrs, pillClass, pickerArmFn)` gained a fourth parameter for this — an optional raw arrow-function expression string (e.g. `` `v => updateItemDateField('<id>','startDate',v)` ``), wired into the calendar icon's own `onclick` via `armPickerCommit()`, omitted for a disabled field (which has no picker to open in the first place — `showPicker()` itself already no-ops there). `onblur` itself is unchanged from the very first fix above (still resets an incomplete edit back to the stored value); `onchange` is gone from every date input's own attribute string entirely. **Testing caveat**: the JXA harness stubs `addEventListener`/`removeEventListener` on its own fake DOM elements as no-ops (see "Tests" above) — `armPickerCommit()`'s own unit tests sidestep that by passing a small hand-rolled plain object standing in for a real `EventTarget` (the same "substitute a minimal fake, not the real DOM mock" technique `writeBackupCopy()`'s own tests already use for `backupDirHandle`), so its listener-arm/fire/remove-once logic runs for real under test; every row-rendering test asserts the correct `armPickerCommit(...)` call is present in each date input's own calendar-icon markup. The actual end-to-end behavior — typing slowly into an already-dated field's year segment with pauses deliberately exceeding the old debounce window, confirming the input never loses focus and nothing commits until blur, then confirming a simulated picker-style commit (a `change` event dispatched after the icon's own click) still lands immediately — was verified with a real headless Chromium instance (Playwright).

**Every inline date field renders as a "pill", not a bare `<input>`.** `dateInputHtml(value, attrs, pillClass, pickerArmFn)` wraps a small calendar icon and the native `<input type="date" class="inline-date-input">` in one `.date-pill` span — this is the single place all of them are built, used identically for item Plan dates, milestone Due/Actual, and (disabled) the before/after pair in a review's change log. The input itself has no visible border/background of its own (the pill provides both); its own native calendar-picker icon is hidden (`::-webkit-calendar-picker-indicator { display: none }`) since the pill's own icon opens the picker instead (`showPicker()`, feature-detected and only wired to the icon specifically, which — for an editable field — also arms `pickerArmFn` via `armPickerCommit()`; see the date-editing bug history below for why). Clicking anywhere else in the pill (its padding/gap, not just the narrow input) also focuses the input, via an `onclick` on the `.date-pill` span itself guarded to skip when the click landed on the `<input>` or `<i>` directly — this exists because `.inline-date-input`'s width is deliberately snug to its `DD.MM.YY` content (no dead trailing space), which leaves less native click-target room than the old wider box did. **Do not set `-webkit-appearance: none` on `.inline-date-input`** to try to shrink it further — it strips Chrome's segmented day/month/year keyboard editing entirely (typing one digit ends up committing/exiting instead of advancing to the next segment); sizing it via a plain explicit `width` is the only safe lever. None of this touches the date *text* itself, which a native `<input type="date">` always renders in whatever format the OS/browser locale uses (`DD.MM.YY` for this author) — only the surrounding chrome changed.

**An unset milestone Actual date starts hidden behind a muted "+" button (`.actual-date-ghost`), not an empty editable pill.** Actual is only meant to be touched when a milestone finishes on a different date than planned — an always-present empty date pill for the common case (finished on time, no override needed) read as a required field waiting to be filled in. `milestoneRowsHtml()` renders the ghost whenever `!m.actualDate && !revealedActualIds.has(m.id)`; clicking it calls `revealActualDate(milestoneId)`, which adds the id to `revealedActualIds` (module-level `Set`, UI-only, not persisted — mirrors `expandedItemIds`), re-renders, and focuses the now-real input (`id="actual-input-${m.id}"`). Pressing Escape while it's revealed but still empty calls `cancelRevealActualDate(milestoneId)`, which removes the id and re-renders back to the ghost, discarding whatever was mid-typed (nothing was saved yet — only `onblur` commits). That Escape handler is only wired up in the revealed-and-still-empty case (`isRevealedEmpty` in `milestoneRowsHtml()`) — once a real `actualDate` exists, the field is a genuine saved value, not a "reveal" state, so there's nothing to cancel back out of. `tests/harness.js`'s `resetState()` resets `revealedActualIds` between tests for the same reason it resets `expandedItemIds` — several test files reuse fixed milestone ids (`'m1'`, `'m2'`) across different tests, so a leaked reveal would silently affect an unrelated later test.

**Manual item order and drag-and-drop reordering.** Every item has an `order: Number`, assigned once at creation (`saveItem()`: `items.filter(i => i.workstreamId === workstreamId).length`, i.e. one past every existing item in that workstream — always the bottom of the list, regardless of due date) and otherwise touched only by dragging. `renderStatusView()`/`renderReview()` both sort by it (see above). The drag handle (`.drag-handle`, a small grip icon, `draggable="true"`) lives inside `.item-actions` next to Edit/Delete — same hover-reveal, same Planning-only gating (hidden during Review, since restructuring order isn't a review action). `.item-actions`'s hover-reveal toggles `visibility`, not `display` — it's always laid out (`display: flex` unconditionally) and only its visibility flips on `.item-row:hover`, specifically so the row never reflows the instant you hover it; toggling `display: none/flex` instead let the column claim its real width (three icon buttons) only on hover, shifting the row every time. `dragStartItem(e, id)` stashes the dragged id in `draggedItemId` (module-level, UI-only, mirroring `expandedItemIds`); the *row* itself (not just the handle) is the drop target (`ondragover`/`ondrop`), so dropping anywhere on a target row works, not just precisely on its own handle. `dropOnItem(e, targetId)` hands off to `reorderItem(fromId, targetId)`, which is a no-op across two different workstreams (dragging isn't how an item's workstream changes — that's still the item modal's job) and otherwise moves the dragged item to the target's position and reassigns clean, contiguous `order` values (`0,1,2,…`) to every item in that workstream — so `order` never needs a repair pass and always exactly matches array position once sorted. `normalizeData()` backfills a missing `order` (legacy data predating this field) from current array position per workstream, in a pass that runs *after* the orphaned-`workstreamId` reassignment above it, so a formerly-orphaned item is never counted against the wrong workstream's counter.
