`unassignedSectionHtml()` renders a `.ws-section` pinned above every real workstream's own section (`renderStatusView()` prepends it: `main.innerHTML = unassignedSectionHtml() + sections + noWorkstreamsHint`) — always shown regardless of the current sidebar filter (`visibleWorkstreams()` only affects the real per-workstream sections below it), since an item sitting there doesn't belong to any workstream yet and shouldn't require switching to "All Workstreams" to see or triage. It holds every item with `workstreamId == null` (`unassignedItemsSorted()`), and below Editor it's omitted entirely once empty — nothing to show and no way to add anything, so there's no point rendering an empty heading. It still shows (read-only, no add affordance) below Editor once there's at least one item in it. This is also the *only* place a brand-new scope item is created — there's no longer a per-workstream "Add item" button (every workstream's own `.ws-section` lost the one it used to have). Its own `.ws-section-header` renders a plain `fa-inbox` icon (an explicit user request) in place of the `.ws-dot` a real workstream's own header shows — there's no workstream color to represent here, so a transparent placeholder dot (the header's original treatment, kept only for grid alignment) was replaced with a real, meaningful icon instead, the same "give this pseudo-section its own icon rather than an empty placeholder" treatment the Journeys page header and the sidebar's own "All Workstreams" row already got.

**Adding an item is a deliberately minimal, name-only inline capture — no modal, no workstream picker.** The section's own "+ Add item" button (`onclick="openInlineQuickAdd()"`) swaps in a plain text input in its place (`unassignedQuickAddOpen`, a UI-only module-level flag, not persisted) rather than opening any dialog. `saveInlineQuickAddItem()` (Enter, or blurring the input) creates the item with **`workstreamId: null`** — "Unassigned", the same idea as the Pending category below, just for workstream instead of category — straight into the one category flagged `pending: true` (`pendingCategory()`/`isPendingCategory(categoryId)`, next to `categoryById()`), seeded with that category's own milestone template — just `['Scope Item Confirmed']` — via the normal `milestonesForCategory()`. Both the item's real workstream *and* its real category are decided together, later, once the scope is actually confirmed (see below) — nothing about either is asked up front.

`cancelInlineQuickAdd()` (Escape) discards whatever was typed and closes the input back to the button, unconditionally. Because closing the input this way (either function calls `render()`, which tears the focused `<input>` out of the DOM) fires that same input's own `onblur` right back — which is wired to `saveInlineQuickAddItem()` — both functions flip `unassignedQuickAddOpen` to `false` *before* calling `render()`, and `saveInlineQuickAddItem()`'s very first line is `if (!unassignedQuickAddOpen) return;`. So whichever call happens first (Enter, or Escape's `cancelInlineQuickAdd()`) does the real work; the second call the resulting blur triggers sees the flag already `false` and no-ops instead of reviving or double-creating anything. Blurring with nothing typed is the same "quietly close, nothing created" outcome, just without an explicit key press.

**A stale/unknown `workstreamId` (a deleted workstream, a broken merge, a hand-edited import) now normalizes to `null`/Unassigned too**, not an arbitrary real workstream (`normalizeData()`: `if (it.workstreamId != null && !wsIds.has(it.workstreamId)) it.workstreamId = null;`) — a genuinely intentional `null` is left alone, but a broken reference surfaces in the Unassigned bucket for a human to actually re-triage, rather than silently landing wherever `workstreams[0]` happens to be. Dashboard's per-workstream rollups (`scopedItems = items.filter(it => wsIds.has(it.workstreamId))`) already exclude anything with no real, valid workstream, so Unassigned items simply don't participate in workstream-scoped stats until they're assigned one — consistent with them not really belonging to a workstream yet.

**The out-of-the-box, real/selectable categories themselves come from `DEFAULT_CATEGORIES`** (top of the `<script>` block, see "Categories (Admin mode)" above) — an array of `{name, milestones}`, one per category (Development, Artefact, Activity, Dependency), sourced from `Milestone_Lookup_Table.xlsx`. `seedDefaults()`/`normalizeData()`'s zero-category fallback both seed straight from it (`DEFAULT_CATEGORIES.map((c, i) => ({..., order: i, dependency: !!c.dependency}))`), so adding, removing, or reordering an out-of-the-box category only ever means editing this one constant.

**The Pending category is reserved, not a normal user category**, even though it lives in the same `categories` array and Admin's category list (with a "Reserved" badge — `renderAdmin()`). `seedDefaults()` always creates it (right after every `DEFAULT_CATEGORIES` entry, at `order: DEFAULT_CATEGORIES.length`) and `normalizeData()` adds one back (`categories.push(...)`) on any save that predates it or has somehow lost it — the quick-add flow assumes exactly one `pending: true` category always exists. `deleteCategoryFromModal()` refuses to delete it outright (see above), and `openScopeAssignModal()`'s category `<select>` filters it out of the choices — it's a one-way waypoint, never a destination to assign *to*.

**Marking the item's "Scope Item Confirmed" milestone Complete is the trigger that asks what it really is.** This is checked by `maybeOfferScopeAssign(it, prevStatus)` — `prevStatus !== 'complete' && it.status === 'complete' && isPendingCategory(it.categoryId)` — called from both places a milestone's status can change into that state: `cycleMilestoneStatus()` (the board's inline click-to-cycle badge, the normal path for a quick-added item with just one milestone) and `saveItem()` (covers editing the milestone's status via the full item modal's dropdown instead). Capturing `prevStatus` before the mutation and only firing on the actual not-complete-to-complete transition means Applying the resulting modal doesn't re-trigger it on some later unrelated save while the item is still sitting at Complete — it only fires again if the status genuinely leaves and re-enters Complete.

`openScopeAssignModal(itemId)` populates a Workstream select (preset to the item's current one — nothing is preselected for a still-Unassigned item, since none of the options match `null`) and a Category select (every category except Pending). **It has no dismiss/"Later" option at all — Apply is the only way to close it** (an explicit user request, "do not allow to later in the assigning step"): the modal's own "Later" button is gone, and `scopeAssignModalBg` is deliberately excluded from both the global Escape-key handler and the generic backdrop-click-to-close wiring at the bottom of the script (every other modal in this app has both) — the same "must complete before closing" treatment the role picker's own mandatory first-launch gate already uses (see "The role picker..." below). It still stays reachable afterward through `itemRowHtml()`'s own status badge — not as a "skip for now" affordance any more, but as a page-reload-recovery path (the page could have been closed/refreshed before Apply was ever clicked): whenever `hasRole('editor') && it.status === 'complete' && isPendingCategory(it.categoryId)`, that badge's `onclick` is `openScopeAssignModal(it.id)` instead of the usual `openItemModal(it.id)` (the pencil "Edit" button in `.item-actions` still opens the full modal regardless, unaffected).

**With zero workstreams in the programme, the "no dismiss" design above turned into a genuine dead end — a user-reported bug.** `populateWorkstreamSelect()` renders a `<select>` with no `<option>`s at all when `workstreams` is empty, and since Apply is the *only* way to close this modal, clicking it was the only option — silently writing an invalid, non-null `workstreamId` (the empty-select sentinel, `''`) onto the item, which then vanished from both the Unassigned bucket and every real workstream section until the next `normalizeData()` pass (load/merge/import) reset the orphaned reference back to `null`. `updateScopeAssignWorkstreamState(selectedId)` fixes this by toggling between the real `#scopeAssignWorkstreamField` and a sibling `#scopeAssignNoWorkstreamsField` — a plain "This programme has no workstreams yet — create one to continue" prompt plus a "+ New workstream" button — based on `workstreams.length`, and disabling `#scopeAssignApplyBtn` outright whenever there's nothing valid to select (the select itself stays in the DOM either way, so the disabled attribute is what actually blocks Apply, not just the prompt covering it visually); `applyScopeAssign()` also refuses to run at all in this state as defense-in-depth, the same disabled-control-plus-function-guard pairing used everywhere else in this app.

**The "+ New workstream" button opens the ordinary workstream modal (`openWorkstreamModal(null)`) on top of the still-open scope-assign modal, rather than requiring the item to be dismissed or half-assigned first.** This is the one place in the whole app two `.modal-bg` overlays are ever deliberately open at once — `#wsModalBg` gets a dedicated `z-index: 60` (every other modal shares the base `z-index: 50`) specifically so it visibly stacks *above* `scopeAssignModalBg`'s own backdrop; without it, DOM order alone (`wsModalBg`'s markup sits earlier in the page) would draw it *behind* instead. Escape/backdrop-click on the nested workstream modal close only that one (`closeWorkstreamModal()`, already excluded — like every path that isn't Apply — from ever touching `scopeAssignModalBg`), dropping back to the still-open scope-assign modal underneath with nothing lost. `saveWorkstream()` gained one line for the "return to caller" half: after the usual save/close/render, `if (scopeAssignItemId) updateScopeAssignWorkstreamState(savedWs.id)` re-populates the scope-assign modal's Workstream field — now that a real one exists — preselecting the workstream just created, and re-enables Apply. This check is safe with no extra flag or nesting-depth tracking: `scopeAssignModalBg` covers the full viewport with no dismiss path, so nothing behind it (including the sidebar's own "+ new workstream" button) is reachable while it's open — the *only* way `openWorkstreamModal()` can ever run while `scopeAssignItemId` is still truthy is through this one nested button.

**A still-Pending item's category can never be changed through the full item modal either — only `applyScopeCategory()` (i.e. this same scope-assign flow) is allowed to move an item out of Pending**, an explicit user request ("prevent change category from unassigned scope items... only the assignment process triggered by completed can change it"). `openItemModal()` disables `#itemCategorySelect` whenever the item being edited currently has `isPendingCategory(it.categoryId)` true — layered on top of, not folded into, the existing role-based disable loop, since this is conditional on the item's own data rather than on who's looking at it, and stays locked even at Editor/Admin. `saveItem()` enforces the same rule server-side as defense-in-depth: for an existing item that's still Pending, `categoryId` is forced to stay whatever it already was, regardless of what the (disabled, but not un-tamperable) select reports.

**The Workstream select gets the identical lock, for the identical reason — a later, explicit user request** ("do not allow to assign a workstream to a pending scope item"). A still-Pending item's `workstreamId` is always `null`/Unassigned (see above); allowing this select to assign a real workstream ahead of `applyScopeCategory()` would let someone half-triage an item — a real workstream, but still the placeholder Pending category and its single-milestone checklist — outside the one flow meant to set both together. `openItemModal()` disables `#itemWorkstreamSelect` under the same `stillPending` flag the Category lock already computes (no second condition needed), and `saveItem()` mirrors `categoryId`'s own defense-in-depth exactly: for a still-Pending item, `workstreamId` is forced to stay whatever it already was (`editingItemForSave.workstreamId`, always `null`) rather than reading the (disabled) select's value.

**A still-Pending item's one "Scope Item Confirmed" milestone can also never be marked Not Applicable** — a related, later explicit user request. Marking it N/A would clear its Due/Actual dates and freeze its status badge to a plain "N/A" label (see "Not Applicable milestones" below) while leaving it permanently uncompletable, stranding the item in Pending forever with no way to trigger `maybeOfferScopeAssign()` at all — the same reasoning already applied to blocking that milestone's *removal* (see `removeMilestoneRow()`'s own Pending guard, and `renderMilestonesEditor()`'s inert Remove button, described above). Blocked at both toggle sites, board and modal, each the same way: `milestoneRowsHtml()`'s `naEditable`/`renderMilestonesEditor()`'s `naToggleHtml` both additionally check `!isPendingCategory(it.categoryId)` (rendering an inert `<span>` instead of a clickable button, same convention as everywhere else in this grid), and `toggleMilestoneNotApplicable()`/`toggleEditingMilestoneNotApplicable()` each refuse outright as defense-in-depth, mirroring `removeMilestoneRow()`'s own guard exactly. The inert span's *off*-state icon is additionally faded to `opacity:.35`, matching the Remove button's own disabled trash icon right next to it — a user-reported inconsistency (the N/A icon read as fully "on" even while genuinely disabled). The *on* state (a milestone that actually is Not Applicable, viewed below Editor) stays full-strength colored regardless — that's a real status worth staying legible, not a disabled control.

**Actual completion date and Dependency are hidden outright in the item modal for a still-Pending item too** — a further explicit user request ("remove for unassigned items External Delivery and Actual Completion date"). Neither is meaningful yet: the item hasn't been scope-assigned to a real workstream/category at all, so there's nothing to record an actual finish date against, and whether it's a dependency isn't a call worth making before the item's real scope is even known. `openItemModal()` computes `stillPending` once, up front (shared with the Category-select lock above), and toggles `#itemActualField`/`#itemDependencyField` display alongside the Journey-only field hides — same "hide outright, don't just disable" treatment. `#itemDependencySpocField` is re-asserted hidden immediately *after* `onItemDependencyChange()` runs (which would otherwise reveal it for the edge case of a Pending item that somehow already has `dependency: true` — an import, a hand-edited file) rather than before, since that call happens later in the same function and would otherwise win. Both fields reappear the moment `applyScopeCategory()` moves the item out of Pending.

**The Pending category's one milestone was renamed from "Scope item confirmed" to "Scope Item Confirmed"** (Title Case) — a cosmetic, explicit user request. It's seeded as a plain name string in two places (`seedDefaults()` and `normalizeData()`'s reseed-if-missing fallback), and nothing in the app branches on the literal string itself (it's never used as a lookup key, only displayed) — so the rename was a two-line data change plus updating comments/tooltip text for consistency, not a schema migration. A pre-existing save with the old lowercase name on an already-created Pending item keeps it as-is (the same "orphaned value is harmless, only new ones get the new name" stance this file already takes elsewhere) — there's no backfill pass for it, since it's cosmetic text, not a value anything compares against.

**That same "nothing matches `null`" gap in `populateWorkstreamSelect()` was a genuine, user-reported bug when it showed up in the *full* item modal instead — `openItemModal()`, not scope-assign.** Editing an already-Unassigned item there and saving *anything at all* — a due date, a name tweak, never touching the Workstream field — silently moved it into `workstreams[0]`: with no `<option>` ever matching a `null` `selectedId`, the browser just defaults an ordinary populated `<select>` to its first option regardless of what the item's real `workstreamId` was, and `saveItem()` read that default straight back out as if the user had actively chosen it. Scope-assign's own copy of this gap (previous paragraph) is fine as-is — forcing a real pick *is* that modal's whole job, an Unassigned item is never staying Unassigned coming out of it. The full edit modal has no such excuse: most edits there have nothing to do with workstream assignment at all. Fixed with a third parameter on `populateWorkstreamSelect(selectedId, elId, includeUnassigned)`: `openItemModal()` passes `true` only when editing an item that's *already* `workstreamId == null`, prepending a matching `<option value="">Unassigned</option>` (`selected`) so the select actually reflects the item's real state instead of silently overriding it; a brand-new item (never Unassigned to begin with — quick-add is the only path into that bucket) and scope-assign's own call both still omit it, unchanged. `saveItem()` reads that empty string back as `document.getElementById('itemWorkstreamSelect').value || null` — every real workstream id is always non-empty, so this only ever converts the Unassigned sentinel back to the same `null` every other Unassigned check in the app already compares against.

"Apply" calls `applyScopeCategory(itemId, workstreamId, categoryId)` — a full swap, not a diff/sync like `applyCategoryMilestoneSync()` (that one reconciles an *existing* category's own template changing over time; this is a one-time jump from the placeholder Pending checklist to a real category's). It sets the item's `workstreamId`/`categoryId` (out of `null`/Unassigned, into a real workstream), tombstones the discarded "Scope Item Confirmed" milestone the same way any other removed milestone is (`deletedMilestoneIds`), replaces `it.milestones` wholesale via `milestonesForCategory(categoryId, it.dueDate)`, and recomputes `it.status`/plan dates from that fresh (all pending) checklist.

## Journeys

**A Journey is a second item *type* — an explicit user request ("build another type next to scope item: 'Journey'") — but not a second data structure.** It's a plain entry in the same `items` array as every scope item, distinguished only by `itemType: 'journey'`. That reuse is deliberate and load-bearing: a Journey gets the row's Edit/Delete actions and `itemRowHtml()`'s entire rendering machinery for free, with no parallel implementation to keep in sync.

**Journeys have two levels — Journey and Sub Journey — a later, explicit user request** ("Journey consists of 2 levels. Journey and sub journey. Scope items can only be attached to subjourneys"), extending the original single-level design rather than replacing it. A **Sub Journey** is a third `itemType` value, `'subjourney'`, sharing every structural trait a top-level Journey has (no category, no workstream, no milestone checklist — see below) plus one field a Journey doesn't need: `parentJourneyId`, the id of the Journey it belongs to. **A scope item now only ever connects to a Sub Journey — never directly to a top-level Journey** — `item.journeyId` (the same field a scope item always used to connect straight to a Journey) is now validated to resolve to an `itemType: 'subjourney'` item specifically; a stale reference to a top-level Journey is treated exactly like a reference to anything else that isn't a valid target, and reset to `null` (see `normalizeData()`'s own backfill, further below). A Sub Journey's own `parentJourneyId` is set once, at creation, and never reassigned afterward — there's no "move this Sub Journey to a different Journey" flow, mirroring how a scope item's own `journeyId` is likewise only ever set by explicitly connecting/disconnecting it, never by an editable field in either object's own modal. Both `journeyId` (on a scope item) and `parentJourneyId` (on a Sub Journey) are always `null` on a Journey/Sub Journey itself, respectively — neither level connects "sideways" or "up" past its own one designated relationship.

**A Journey/Sub Journey has no category, no workstream, and no milestone checklist at all — an explicit user simplification** ("do not add milestones to it... you can remove the Journey category completely"), stripping out what an earlier version of this feature had: a reserved "Journey" category with a fixed 4-milestone template and a milestone-completion trigger — carried over unchanged to the Sub Journey level once it was added, since the same reasoning (nothing here to seed a checklist from) applies just as much one level down. `categoryId` is `null` for either (every other item still needs a real one) — `normalizeData()`'s categoryId backfill runs *after* the `itemType` backfill specifically so it can skip a Journey/Sub Journey outright rather than repairing its `null` into `categories[0].id` the way a plain item's genuinely-missing categoryId is. The item modal hides its Workstream (`#itemWorkstreamField`), Category (`#itemCategoryField`), and Milestones (`#itemMilestonesField`) fields outright whenever the item being created/edited is either one — not left unpopulated, disabled, or showing an empty editor with nothing to add, since none of the three apply. `saveItem()` forces both `workstreamId` and `categoryId` to `null` for either regardless of whatever the (hidden) fields report. `openItemModal()`'s own title text (`Edit Journey`/`New Journey`/`Edit Sub Journey`/`New Sub Journey`/`Edit item`/`New item`) is driven off the same three-way `editingType` this whole feature is now built on — see the modal's own paragraph, below.

**Neither a Journey nor a Sub Journey is ever assigned to a workstream at all — an explicit user correction** ("Journey should not be assigned to workstreams. They are overarching"), carried down to the Sub Journey level once it existed. `workstreamId` is always `null` for either, and unlike an Unassigned scope item (`workstreamId: null` too, but meaning "awaiting triage" — see "Quick-add scope items..." above), that `null` is permanent for both, not a placeholder. Two consequences follow directly:
- `unassignedItemsSorted()` (the Planning board's own Unassigned bucket) explicitly excludes `itemType === 'journey'` **and** `itemType === 'subjourney'` even though all three share `workstreamId == null` — a Journey/Sub Journey belongs in its own dedicated view, not mixed into a bucket that's specifically for scope items awaiting a real workstream. `buildScopeItemsSheet()` (the Excel export's Scope Items sheet) excludes both from its own Unassigned section the same way, for the same reason.
- **Journeys is no longer its own mode at all — it's Planning's second sub-tab (`planningTab`), alongside the status board itself ("Scope Items") — a final, explicit user request** ("move Journeys to All Workstreams / Planning / Journeys. The current page from All Workstreams / Planning should move to All Workstreams / Planning / Scope Items"). It's a single flat list (`allJourneys()`, sorted by `order`), not one section per workstream the way Planning's own status board is — always rendered in one `.ws-section.journeys-list` card, whether or not it's currently narrowed by the shared workstream selector (see `journeysForCurrentFilter()`, point 9 below, for that narrowing). Getting here took several stops, kept below as history: Journeys started with its own sidebar-less container (`#journeysBody`) and its own topbar button (`#tabJourneys`, sitting outside `.view-tabs` next to `#tabAdmin`) — the same "not workstream-scoped, so a shared sidebar filter would be a dead, misleading control" reasoning Admin mode still uses (see "Modes" above). A later, explicit user request ("move journeys in the left navigation bar, separate it from workstreams") moved its entry point into the sidebar instead, and it went through a few more sidebar reshuffles after that (points 1-7), before finally landing inside Planning as a sub-tab (point 8), which further requests then made workstream-aware (point 9) and searchable (point 10):
  1. First landed as its own labeled section — a `sidebar-label` "Journeys" heading plus a single `#journeysNav` row, sitting *above* the "Workstreams" heading + `#wsList` (a follow-up "move journey over workstream" request swapped what had originally been the reverse order).
  2. A later, explicit user request ("add External Delivery to the left navigation pane, under 'All Workstreams'") added a third nav entry, initially as the 2nd row inside `#wsList` itself (under "All Workstreams", ahead of the real workstream rows) — see "Dependencies" above for that row's own history.
  3. A further explicit user request ("All Workstream on top, Journey underneath, then a separation line, then workstreams") unified all three into one ordered top group, `#topNavList` — **All Workstreams → Journeys → External Delivery** at the time (later swapped to Journeys → All Workstreams — see point 5 below), then a separator, then the "Workstreams" label + `#wsList` (now holding *only* the real, per-workstream rows). This is the current shape: `renderSidebar()` builds `#topNavList`'s entire innerHTML in one call (all three rows, each with its own `active` state and click target — `selectWorkstreamFromSidebar(null)`, `setMode('journeys')`, `setMode('external')` respectively), reusing the `.ws-row`/`.ws-row-name`/`.ws-row-count` classes a workstream row uses (a route icon for Journeys, a globe for External Delivery, in place of a color dot), then separately builds `#wsList` from `workstreams.map(...)` alone. The old standalone `#journeysNav` container and "Journeys" heading are gone.
  4. **The separator itself needed a follow-up fix — a user-reported "I don't see a line."** It first shipped as a `border-bottom` (`.ws-row-group-end`) on whichever row happened to render last in the group (External Delivery, given the order above), mirroring `.ws-row-all`'s own original bottom-border. Verified by screenshot: this rendered, but read as the bottom edge of *one row's own button* rather than a real section break — `.ws-row`'s `border-radius: 7px` rounded the border's corners, and the row's own horizontal padding inset it a few pixels from the sidebar's edges, so it never looked like a plain, deliberate dividing line the way a real `<hr>` would. Replaced with a dedicated static element instead, `<div class="sidebar-divider"></div>`, sitting in the HTML shell between `#topNavList` and the "Workstreams" label (`.sidebar-divider { height: 1px; background: var(--border); margin: 10px 4px 0; }`) — a plain, un-rounded, edge-to-edge line, immune to the top group's own row order ever changing again since nothing about it depends on which row renders last. The "Workstreams" label's own `margin-top: 16px` (which used to supply the gap below the old border-bottom) was removed once the divider took over that job — and the divider's own bottom margin is deliberately `0` (a follow-up user request, "remove the empty space between the separation line and workstreams"), relying purely on `.sidebar-label`'s own `8px` top padding for breathing room rather than adding a second, stacked gap on top of it.
  5. **A later, explicit user request ("switch journeys and all workstream in the navigation bar") swapped the group's first two rows** — Journeys now sits *above* "All Workstreams", i.e. `#topNavList`'s order at the time became **Journeys → All Workstreams → External Deliveries**, then the separator, then the real workstream list. This was a pure reorder of the three `<div>`s already being built in one `innerHTML` assignment — no change to any row's own markup, click target, or `active`-class logic, and the dedicated `.sidebar-divider` static element (see the previous point) meant this reorder needed no companion CSS change either, unlike the old `border-bottom`-on-the-last-row approach would have required.
  6. **A later, separate explicit user request moved External Deliveries out of this group entirely, into its own topbar button (`#tabExternal`) — see "Dependencies" above for the full change, including the scoping behavior that moved with it.** `#topNavList` dropped to two rows, **Journeys → All Workstreams**. `renderSidebar()`'s own `inWorkstreamScope` gate — which used to exclude both `'journeys'` and `'external'` from ever showing a workstream row as active — dropped to excluding only `'journeys'`, since External Deliveries genuinely reflected the shared selector by this point and no longer needed that suppression.
  7. **A separate explicit user request removed External Deliveries as a standalone mode/page altogether**, folding it into a Planning-only quick-filter chip instead (see "Dependencies" above) — `#tabExternal` and `mode === 'external'` are both gone now, not just moved again. This had no further effect on `#topNavList` or `inWorkstreamScope` beyond what point 6 already left in place: **Journeys → All Workstreams** remained the shape of this group for the rest of this history, and `inWorkstreamScope`'s exclusion of `'journeys'` alone (never having anything to do with External Deliveries by this point) needed no further change.
  8. **A final, separate explicit user request removed the sidebar row/mode concept for Journeys too, folding it into Planning as a second sub-tab instead** — mirroring how External Deliveries' own standalone mode/page was folded into a Planning quick-filter chip (point 7), just as a sub-tab rather than a chip, since Journeys is a whole page's worth of content, not a single boolean filter. `mode === 'journeys'`, `MODES`' own entry for it, and the sidebar's Journeys row are all gone; `#topNavList` is back to a single row, "All Workstreams". In their place: `planningTab` (`'scope' | 'journeys'`, module-level, UI-only, not persisted — the exact same shape `reviewTab` already established for Review's own sub-tabs), `setPlanningTab(t)`, and a new static toolbar, `#planningModeToolbar` (a `.view-tabs` block, `#tabPlanningScope`/`#tabPlanningJourneys`), shown only for `mode === 'planning'`, sitting above the existing `#planningSearchToolbar`. `renderMain()` is the actual dispatch point: `planningTab === 'journeys'` calls `renderJourneys()` (itself unchanged — it still just writes the same markup into `#main`), anything else renders the status board exactly as before. `#planningSearchToolbar` (the scope-item search box plus the status/dependency chips) is now additionally gated on `planningTab === 'scope'` — the Journey/Sub Journey tree has no use for a flat item search or status filter, so it's hidden whenever the Journeys sub-tab is showing rather than sitting there uselessly. At this point `renderSidebar()` still had its own `inWorkstreamScope` gate (originally `mode !== 'journeys'`, now rephrased `!(mode === 'planning' && planningTab === 'journeys')`) suppressing a workstream row's `active` state while the Journeys sub-tab was showing — see point 9 below for why that gate is gone entirely now. `selectWorkstreamFromSidebar(id)` also now forces `planningTab = 'scope'` alongside its existing `mode = 'planning'` — picking a workstream (or "All Workstreams") is a request to see that selection's own scope items, so it should land there regardless of which Planning sub-tab was showing beforehand. `render()`'s generic `MODES.forEach(...)` id-toggle loop needed no exclusion for `'journeys'` any more either, since it's simply not in `MODES` — the loop is back to its plain, unfiltered form.
  9. **A further, separate explicit user request made the Journeys sub-tab itself workstream-aware for the first time** ("In a workstream (not all workstream) show under Journeys only the Journeys where respective scope items of that workstream are included") — reversing the "always ignores the shared selector entirely" stance every version up to this point had taken. `journeysForCurrentFilter()` is the actual narrowing: with `filterWorkstreamId` null ("All Workstreams"), it returns `allJourneys()` unfiltered, same as always; with one specific workstream selected, it keeps only the Journeys that actually *reach into* that workstream — a Journey qualifies if any one of its own connected Sub Journeys (`connectedSubJourneys()`) has at least one connected scope item (`connectedScopeItems()`) whose own `workstreamId` matches. This is a display-only narrowing, the same "narrows what's listed, never anything about the underlying data" precedent Planning's own search box/status chips already established (see "Status board" above) — a Journey/Sub Journey is still never itself assigned to a workstream (unchanged, see above); this just decides which ones are worth showing while looking at one workstream in particular. A Journey with no Sub Journeys at all, or with Sub Journeys that have nothing connected, is filtered out of every workstream-scoped view the same way one with no *matching* items is — there's nothing tying it to any specific workstream to show. A Journey whose different Sub Journeys each reach into a *different* workstream legitimately shows up under each of those workstreams individually, not just one — the check is "does *any* connected item match", not "do *all* of them". `renderJourneys()` reads `journeysForCurrentFilter()` in place of `allJourneys()`, and — since the list can now genuinely be empty while non-empty overall — its "nothing yet" placeholder branches on whether a workstream is currently scoping it: `"No journeys yet."` when unfiltered, `"No Journeys with scope items in \"<workstream name>\" yet."` when scoped and empty. The section header's own title also grows a `" — <workstream name>"` suffix while scoped (`Journeys — Alpha`), the same "make the scoping visible, not just its effect" reasoning Planning's own per-workstream section headers already get for free from their own name/color — this flat list has no such per-row real estate to borrow, so the header carries it instead. `journeysExpandAllIds()` was updated to read `journeysForCurrentFilter()` too, for the same "never act on what a filter is currently narrowing out" reason `expandAllToggleHtml()` already follows on Planning's own side. **`renderSidebar()`'s `inWorkstreamScope` gate is removed outright, not just re-keyed again** — now that the Journeys sub-tab genuinely reacts to `filterWorkstreamId`, suppressing a workstream row's `active` highlight while that sub-tab was showing would itself be the misleading behavior (the selection has a real, visible effect there now); both `filterWorkstreamId === null` and `filterWorkstreamId === w.id` are back to being the only conditions gating a row's `active` class, same as any other mode.
  10. **A further, separate explicit user request gave the Journeys sub-tab its own search box** ("build a search feature for journeys... similar like for planning") — `#journeysSearchInput`/`journeysSearchQuery`/`setJourneysSearch()`/`clearJourneysSearch()` mirror Planning's own `#planningSearchInput`/`planningSearchQuery`/`setPlanningSearch()`/`clearPlanningSearch()` exactly (a new static toolbar, `#journeysSearchToolbar`, sitting outside `#main` for the identical "never steal focus from the input mid-keystroke" reason, shown only for `planningTab === 'journeys'`). `matchesJourneysSearch(j)` reaches one level deeper than a plain name check, though: a Journey matches if its own name does, **or** if any one of its own Sub Journeys' names does — Sub Journeys are a second, equally-named tier in this tree that's just as reasonable to search for directly, unlike Planning's own search box, which has only one level (a scope item's own name) to check. It deliberately doesn't reach a third level, into connected scope items' own names — the same "quick find, not exhaustive" boundary `matchesPlanningSearch()` itself draws just short of milestone names. `journeysForCurrentFilter()` now composes the workstream filter (point 9) and the search query as a plain AND, in that order — searching a workstream that a Journey doesn't reach into can't resurrect it. `renderJourneys()` computes the workstream-filtered list and the final (workstream + search) list as two separate steps specifically so its empty-state message can tell "nothing here for this workstream at all" apart from "a search query found nothing among what genuinely is here" (`No Journeys match "<query>".`) — conflating the two would misreport a real, empty-but-searchable list as if the workstream had nothing in it. `journeysExpandAllIds()` reads the same fully-composed `journeysForCurrentFilter()`, so Expand-all still only ever acts on what a search (in addition to the workstream filter) is currently narrowing out.
  11. **A further, separate explicit user request gave the Journeys sub-tab its own quick-filter status chips too** ("add search pills to the newly added searches") — `#journeysStatusFilterChips`/`journeysStatusFilters`/`setJourneysStatusFilter()`/`matchesJourneysStatusFilter()` mirror Planning's own `planningStatusFilters`/`setPlanningStatusFilter()` exactly, multi-select from the start (unlike Planning's own chips, which only became multi-select later — see "Status board" above). Matched against a top-level Journey's own computed status (`computeJourneyStatus(j.id) || 'not-started'`), not a Sub Journey's or connected scope item's own status — the chips filter which *Journeys* show, the same level every other narrowing step in `journeysForCurrentFilter()` operates at. `journeysForCurrentFilter()` composes all three steps (workstream filter, search, status chips) as a plain AND, in that order. `renderJourneys()`'s empty-state message now has three cases: the pre-existing workstream-scoped ones when nothing exists at all for the current scope, `No Journeys match "<query>".` when search alone (or search plus chips) finds nothing, and `No Journeys match the selected filters.` when chips alone are active and find nothing (reworded from an earlier, narrower `...selected status filter(s).` once point 12 below gave the chip row a second, non-status filter type — the generic wording matches Action Log/Decision Log's own identical fallback, see "Action Log and Decision Log also each grew their own quick-filter chip row" above). `renderJourneysStatusChips()` is called unconditionally alongside `renderPlanningStatusChips()` in `render()`, same "cheap enough, no mode check needed at the call site" reasoning.
  12. **A further, separate explicit user request added a Dependencies toggle chip to this same row too** ("add dependencies as search pill for journeys, like in workstreams") — `journeysDependencyFilter`/`setJourneysDependencyFilter()`/`matchesJourneysDependencyFilter()` mirror Planning's own `planningDependencyFilter`/`setPlanningDependencyFilter()`/`matchesPlanningDependencyFilter()` exactly: a plain on/off toggle (not multi-select, since `dependency` is itself a boolean with nothing to select between), appended to `renderJourneysStatusChips()`'s own output after a `.chip-divider`, same shape as Planning's own status-chips-then-Dependencies-chip row. The one real difference is what it's checked against: a Journey/Sub Journey has no `dependency` field of its own at all (that's a scope-item-only concept — see "Dependencies" above), so `matchesJourneysDependencyFilter(j)` reaches down through the same two hops `journeysAfterWorkstreamFilter()`'s own workstream check already does — a Journey qualifies if any of its connected Sub Journeys has any connected scope item flagged `dependency: true`. `journeysForCurrentFilter()` composes it as a fourth, final AND step after the status chips.

**Creating a Journey is a name-only inline capture, matching the Unassigned section's own "+ Add item" quick-add exactly** (`openJourneyQuickAdd()`/`cancelJourneyQuickAdd()`/`saveJourneyQuickAddItem()`, `journeyQuickAddOpen` module-level UI flag — see "Quick-add scope items, the Unassigned bucket, and the Pending category" above) — an explicit user request, replacing `renderJourneys()`'s own "+ Add Journey" button opening the full item modal (`openItemModal(null, null, 'journey')`). A Journey has nothing else worth asking for up front anyway (no workstream, no category, no milestones — see below), so the modal was never collecting more than a name here in practice; this just skips opening it. `saveJourneyQuickAddItem()` builds the same shape `saveItem()`'s own Journey-creation branch does (`itemType:'journey'`, `workstreamId`/`categoryId` forced `null`, no milestones, default green tags, `'not-started'` status), including the same `items.filter(i => i.workstreamId === null).length` order computation — so a Journey created either way sits at a comparable position. `openItemModal(null, null, 'journey')` itself is untouched and still reachable — it's still what editing an *existing* Journey opens (via the row's own Edit pencil), and its `presetItemType` parameter still exists for that path — only the creation entry point moved. `editingItemType` (module-level, now `'scope'` | `'journey'` | `'subjourney'`) is still the single source of truth `openItemModal()` sets for both the new-item and edit-existing-item cases (`it ? it.itemType : (presetItemType === 'journey' || presetItemType === 'subjourney' ? presetItemType : 'scope')`) and that `saveItem()` reads back (captured into a local `savingType`, with `savingJourneyLike = savingType === 'journey' || savingType === 'subjourney'`, before `closeItemModal()` resets `editingItemType` back to `'scope'` for next time) — used to force `categoryId`/`workstreamId` to `null` regardless of what the (hidden) fields report, and, only on the "creating new" branch, to stamp `itemType` itself (never part of the shared `data` object also used for edits, the same "brand-new-only default, never resets on a plain re-save" treatment `itStatus`/`businessStatus`/`budgetStatus` already get).

**Creating a Sub Journey is the same name-only inline capture, one level down — the only way one is ever created at all, since there's no "+ Add Sub Journey" path through the full item modal either.** `openSubJourneyQuickAdd(journeyId)`/`cancelSubJourneyQuickAdd()`/`saveSubJourneyQuickAddItem()` mirror the Journey-level trio exactly, except the open flag is scoped per-parent rather than a plain boolean: `subJourneyQuickAddOpenFor` (module-level, the parent Journey's own id, or `null`) rather than `journeyQuickAddOpen`, since the input itself has to render *inside* whichever Journey's own row happens to be expanded (`itemRowHtml()`'s `subJourneyQuickAddHtml`, appended after the list of connected Sub Journeys), not in one single fixed place the way `renderJourneys()`'s own flat list has room for. `saveSubJourneyQuickAddItem()` builds the identical shape `saveJourneyQuickAddItem()` does, plus its own `parentJourneyId: journeyId` and `itemType: 'subjourney'` — `journeyId` itself stays `null` on the newly-created Sub Journey, same as it always is at creation for every item type; only a *scope item* ever gets a non-null `journeyId`, and only via `toggleJourneyConnection()`.

**The row badge — removed.** `itemRowHtml()`'s Name cell used to get a small icon prefix (`.journey-type-badge`, `journeyBadgeHtml`) — `fa-route` for a Journey, a distinct `fa-diagram-project` for a Sub Journey — not a dedicated grid column, since either is a plain item under the hood and this was the only thing telling its row apart from an ordinary scope item's (or, for a Sub Journey nested under its own parent, from that parent's row) wherever they appeared in the same list. Removed outright per an explicit user request ("remove the icons for Journey and SubJourney in list view") — indentation (`.item-row-indent-1`/`-2`, see below) and each row's own position in the hierarchy are enough to tell them apart now; the row itself renders exactly like an ordinary scope item's. **The Journeys page's own headline icon is a separate, unrelated thing and was kept** — `renderJourneys()`'s own `.ws-section-header` still shows a single `fa-route` icon next to the page's "Journeys" title (not per-row, just once for the whole list), the same way every other section header in this app (a workstream's own colored dot, Unassigned's inbox icon) gets one representative icon.

**Nesting is visual, via plain extra `padding-left` — an explicit user request ("in journey view, indent subjourney").** `.item-row-indent-1` (34px) marks a Sub Journey's own row, nested under its parent Journey; `.item-row-indent-2` (54px) marks a Sub Journey's connected scope items, nested one level further. `itemRowHtml()`'s `indentClass` picks between the two (`isSubJourney ? 'item-row-indent-1' : viewOnly ? 'item-row-indent-2' : ''`) — a plain scope item outside this hierarchy (Planning's own status board) gets neither. **A later, separate explicit user request extended the same indent to an expanded connected item's own milestone header/rows** ("indent the milestones and headers in alignment with the Sub journey") — `milestoneHeaderHtml()`/`milestoneRowsHtml()` both take the same `viewOnly` flag `itemRowHtml()` already threads through them, and add `item-row-indent-2` to their own `.milestone-header`/`.milestone-sub-row` root element whenever it's set, matching the connected item's own row exactly (milestones never appear at the `item-row-indent-1` level, since a Sub Journey has no milestones of its own — see above — only the `-2` case is ever actually reachable, though the CSS covers both for symmetry). This needed its own explicit compound selectors (`.milestone-header.item-row-indent-1`/`.milestone-sub-row.item-row-indent-2` etc.), not just relying on `.item-row-indent-1`/`-2` alone: those two rules are defined once, early in the stylesheet, right after `.item-row` itself, and `.milestone-header`'s/`.milestone-sub-row`'s own full `padding` shorthand (necessarily defined later, since it's a different, later class block) would otherwise win the cascade and reset `padding-left` straight back to the base 14px regardless of which indent class was also present.

**A design discussion on telling Journey/Sub Journey/scope item rows apart now that per-row icons are gone produced three ideas; only the first survived.** A further explicit user request ("build 1 and 2") layered a tree guide-line and a Journey "section header" tint on top of the plain padding-left indent above; a later, separate explicit user request ("remove 2") removed the guide-line again (it was a `position:relative` + `::before` pseudo-element pair on `.item-row-indent-1`/`.item-row-indent-2`, fully gone now, not just hidden). A third explicit user request ("build 3") then added a three-tier typography step-down (`.item-row-indent-2 .item-name { font-weight: 500; font-size: 13px; }`, scoped to a Sub Journey's own connected scope item — the leaf level); a final explicit user request ("revert 3") removed that too. What's left, below, is idea #1 alone — the Journey header tint.

**Journey "header" tint**: a top-level Journey's own row gets one more class, `item-row-journey-header` (`itemRowHtml()`'s `journeyHeaderClass`, set only when `isJourney`) — `background: var(--panel-2)` plus taller `padding-top`/`padding-bottom` (12px, matching `.ws-section-header`'s own) and a bolder, slightly larger `.item-name` (700/14.5px vs the base 600/14px, inherited from `body`'s own `font-size`). This mirrors `.ws-section-header`'s own "this groups what's below it" treatment for a workstream, so a Journey reads the same way structurally without needing its own icon — deliberately **not** applied to a Sub Journey's own row, which stays a plain row like any other, since only the top-level container is meant to read as a header.

**Journeys also has its own "Expand all"/"Collapse all" control now — an explicit user request to bring Planning's own per-section control (see "Status board" above) here too.** `journeysExpandAllIds()`/`journeysExpandAllToggleHtml()` are `expandAllToggleHtml()`'s own counterparts, not a reuse of it directly, since "expandable" means something genuinely different in this hierarchy: a Journey/Sub Journey is *always* expandable, even with zero connections (`expandAllToggleHtml()`'s own `it.milestones.length > 0` filter would find nothing at all here, since neither ever has milestones — see above), and there are two nested levels to unfold rather than Planning's single level of milestone nesting. `journeysExpandAllIds()` walks every top-level Journey (`allJourneys()`) plus every one of its own Sub Journeys (`connectedSubJourneys()`) into one flat id list — deliberately stopping there: a Sub Journey's own connected scope items are revealed (their rows show up once their parent Sub Journey is expanded), but those items' own milestone checklists stay collapsed, the same "one more click for genuinely deep detail" boundary already drawn elsewhere in this app. `journeysExpandAllToggleHtml()` renders the identical icon-only `.row-icon-btn` markup `expandAllToggleHtml()` does (same `fa-angles-down`/`fa-angles-up`, same aggregate-state-driven title), positioned the same way — at the very start of `renderJourneys()`'s own page header, right before the `fa-route` icon and "Journeys" title — and both funnel into the same shared `toggleExpandAllForItems(ids)`, which is what required that function to move from `renderMain()` to the general `render()` (see "Status board" above) once it became reachable from a second mode.

**A Sub Journey connects to *existing* scope items — the reverse of an earlier version of this feature, which instead decomposed a Journey into brand-new ones.** `item.journeyId` (`String|null`) is the link — set on the *scope item*, pointing at the Sub Journey it belongs to, so one Sub Journey can have many connected scope items but a scope item belongs to at most one Sub Journey at a time. `connectedScopeItems(journeyId)` returns every item whose `journeyId` matches, sorted by `order` — the function's name predates the Sub Journey level (it used to be called with a top-level Journey's own id, back when that was the only level that existed) and is now only ever called with a Sub Journey's id, but was left unrenamed rather than touched purely cosmetically. **A top-level Journey no longer connects directly to scope items at all** — that's what the Sub Journey level exists for — so `itemRowHtml()`'s `connectBtnHtml` (a link icon in `.item-actions`, right next to the drag handle, Editor+) only ever renders whenever `it.itemType === 'subjourney'`, wired to `openJourneyConnectModal(it.id)`; a Journey's own row gets no connect icon at all any more.

**Since a Journey's own connections are now one level of indirection removed (Journey → Sub Journey → scope items, not Journey → scope items directly), so is how it "gathers" scope items**: `connectedSubJourneys(journeyId)` is `connectedScopeItems()`'s counterpart at the Journey level — every item whose `parentJourneyId` matches and whose `itemType` is exactly `'subjourney'`, sorted by `order` the same way. Nothing calls `connectedScopeItems()` with a top-level Journey's own id any more; the only two starting points into the connection graph are `connectedSubJourneys(journeyId)` (Journey → its Sub Journeys) and `connectedScopeItems(subJourneyId)` (a Sub Journey → its scope items).

**The connect modal** (`journeyConnectModalBg`) lists every scope item not already claimed by a *different* Sub Journey, grouped by workstream (plus an "Unassigned" group) the same way Planning's own status board is — familiar structure rather than an undifferentiated flat list. Each row is a checkbox, checked when `it.journeyId` already equals the Sub Journey being managed. Every function here (`journeyConnectItemId`, `openJourneyConnectModal()`, `renderJourneyConnectList()`, `toggleJourneyConnection()`) predates the Sub Journey level and still says "journey" throughout its own names — kept as-is rather than renamed wholesale (the same "internal name doesn't need to track a display-only relabel" precedent Admin/Settings mode already sets — see "Categories (Admin mode)" above) — but every one of them now only ever operates on a Sub Journey's own id, never a top-level Journey's; `openJourneyConnectModal()`'s own modal title reflects this explicitly (`Connect scope items to Sub Journey "<name>"`, not just `"<name>"`) so the distinction is visible in the UI even though it isn't in the function names. **`renderJourneyConnectList()`'s own candidates filter excludes both `itemType === 'journey'` and `itemType === 'subjourney'`, not just the one that used to exist** — a user-reported bug ("linking scope items does not show all available scope items") originally traced to a stricter `=== 'scope'` check excluding any item whose `itemType` hadn't been backfilled yet, since only `normalizeData()` (load/merge/import — see "Data integrity" below) ever does that backfill, not a plain `save()`. The concrete case: `saveInlineQuickAddItem()` (see "Quick-add scope items..." below) never stamped `itemType`/`journeyId` on the item it created at all, so a scope item quick-added earlier in the same session stayed invisible here until the next full reload. Fixed on both ends — `saveInlineQuickAddItem()` now stamps `itemType: 'scope', journeyId: null` explicitly, matching every other item-creation path in this file (`saveItem()` always sets `itemType` at creation) — and the filter itself switched to the same defensive `!==` form `unassignedItemsSorted()`/`buildScopeItemsSheet()` already use, extended to cover the new `'subjourney'` value once it existed too, so an item that somehow still arrives with no `itemType` (an old import, a hand-edited file) is included here too rather than silently dropped from this one picker, while a genuine Journey or Sub Journey is correctly excluded either way. Unlike the item modal's own "detached working copy, committed on Save" editors, this one writes straight to `items` the instant a checkbox changes (`toggleJourneyConnection(itemId, checked)` sets `it.journeyId` to the Sub Journey's id or `null` and immediately `save()`s) — there's no natural "row" to stage here (the list is just every existing scope item), and every toggle is trivially reversible by unchecking again, so a deferred commit wouldn't actually protect against anything. `toggleJourneyConnection()` calls the top-level `render()` (so the Journeys/Planning views underneath stay current) and then re-renders the modal's own list separately (`renderJourneyConnectList()`) — `render()` doesn't reach into an open modal's own body, and a just-toggled item might now need to disappear from (or appear correctly checked in) the candidate list itself. "Done" (`closeJourneyConnectModal()`) just closes the modal — there's nothing pending to discard, every toggle already committed.

**The link is surfaced, read-only, on both sides of each relationship, in the item modal.** A connected scope item shows `itemJourneyLinkField`/`itemJourneyLinkBadge` naming the Sub Journey it belongs to (label "From Sub Journey"); a Sub Journey itself shows the identical field naming its own parent Journey instead (label "From Journey"), reading `parentJourneyId` rather than `journeyId` — both share one field/badge pair in the modal's markup, with `openItemModal()` picking which relationship (and which label text, via `#itemJourneyLinkLabel`) applies based on the item being edited: `it.itemType === 'scope' && it.journeyId` for the first case, `it.itemType === 'subjourney' && it.parentJourneyId` for the second. Either way it's purely informational: nothing in the item modal itself ever writes to `journeyId` (only `toggleJourneyConnection()` does) or to `parentJourneyId` (only set once, at Sub Journey creation).

**A Journey's own row is expandable to reveal its connected Sub Journeys; a Sub Journey's own row is expandable to reveal its connected scope items — the same chevron interaction a scope item's milestone checklist already uses, one level down from where it used to be.** `itemRowHtml()`'s chevron is normally only clickable when an item has at least one milestone (`total`); for either level it's clickable unconditionally (`expandable = total || isJourney || isSubJourney`), even with zero connections, since there's no other on-row hint that expanding it does anything. `toggleItemExpanded()` — the same function, and the same `expandedItemIds` set, milestone checklists already use — drives both; no Journey/Sub-Journey-specific expand state was needed. A Journey with nothing connected yet shows a plain "No Sub Journeys yet." placeholder row (plus its own inline "+ Add Sub Journey" affordance right below it — see above); a Sub Journey with nothing connected shows "No scope items connected yet." — either way expanding something that hasn't been connected to anything yet still visibly confirms there's nothing there (as opposed to looking identical to a collapse). The row's own `milestone-count-badge` cell, empty for a zero-milestone scope item, instead shows the connected count — "N sub journeys" for a Journey, "N scope items" for a Sub Journey — whenever it's non-zero, the same "always a real element" column-alignment discipline every other cell in this row already follows (see "Column layout is a real CSS grid" above), just with type-specific content.

**A Journey's connected Sub Journeys render as real, fully-interactive `itemRowHtml()` rows — not `viewOnly` — since managing them (renaming, deleting, connecting scope items to one) is the whole point of expanding a Journey; a Sub Journey's own connected scope items render `viewOnly`, exactly the way a top-level Journey's connections always did in the original single-level design.** `itemRowHtml(it, reviewCycle, viewOnly)`'s third parameter drives both: for a Journey, `subRows` maps `subJourneys.map(sj => itemRowHtml(sj))` — no `viewOnly` — plus the Sub-Journey quick-add row described above; for a Sub Journey, `subRows` maps `connected.map(ci => itemRowHtml(ci, null, true))`, an explicit user request ("show scope items like this under a journey") replacing an earlier version that rendered a custom, stripped-down Scope Item/Due/Workstream/Status line via now-deleted `connectedItemsHeaderHtml()`/`connectedItemsRowsHtml()` functions. Both are safe from infinite recursion: a Sub Journey's own connected items are always `itemType: 'scope'` (the connect modal only ever offers scope items), so their own `isJourney`/`isSubJourney` checks are always false, and a Journey never connects to another Journey (only to Sub Journeys, which never connect back up to more than one Journey each). Three things follow from reusing the real row rather than a summary, for the `viewOnly` (Sub Journey → scope item) case specifically:
- **Stays collapsed by default** — reusing `itemRowHtml()` means a connected item's own chevron/expand state is real too (governed by the same `expandedItemIds` set), so its own milestone checklist, if it has one, doesn't unfold automatically just because the parent Sub Journey did; a user has to expand it separately, same as anywhere else in the app.
- **`viewOnly`, not just `reviewCycle`'s partial read-only mode, forces every editing affordance off regardless of role.** `const editable = !viewOnly && hasRole('editor');` (in `itemRowHtml()`, `milestoneRowsHtml()`) is the single flag every interactive control in these rows now checks instead of calling `hasRole('editor')` directly — the IT/Business/Budget tag badges, the inline Start/Due date pills, and the Actions cell (drag handle, Connect, Edit, Delete — all omitted, `.item-actions` rendered as a real-but-empty span for the usual column-collapse reason) all fall back to their existing below-Editor read-only rendering, just unconditionally. This is a distinct concept from `reviewCycle`'s own partial read-only mode (which still leaves Actual dates and the milestone status badge editable, plus adds a review-confirm column) — the two parameters are never passed together in current usage.
- **"MS Req." is omitted, not just disabled, when a connected item's own milestones are expanded.** `milestoneHeaderHtml(reviewCycle, viewOnly)`'s "MS Req." label becomes an empty string (the `<span>` cell itself stays, per the same column-alignment invariant) when `viewOnly`; `milestoneRowsHtml()`'s Not Applicable toggle button is omitted outright (not rendered as an inert span the way it is below Editor) for the same reason — there's nothing to toggle in a read-only context, so unlike the tag badges/date pills (which still show their value, just not editable) this control has no value worth showing at all.

**The Journeys list overrides `--item-grid-cols` for its own container, scoped via a `.journeys-list` wrapper class, rather than changing the shared global default Planning/Review still depend on.** A user-reported bug — "action item[s] reach out of the border" — traced (via direct `getBoundingClientRect()` measurement) to `.item-actions` being a plain flex row with no width cap: at anything narrower than roughly 1150px, its 4 icons (drag/connect/edit/delete — one more than a plain scope item's row ever has, thanks to Connect) overflowed the shared grid's 56px Actions track, and neither `.item-row` nor `.ws-section` clips overflow (see "Status board" above), so the icons visibly spilled past the card's own right edge instead of being hidden or scrolled to. This governs the whole `.journeys-list` container regardless of which row within it has the most icons — today that's a Sub Journey's own row (drag/connect/edit/delete), since a top-level Journey's own row lost its Connect icon once Sub Journeys took over that role (down to drag/edit/delete, one fewer than before), but the width fix itself is still sized for 4, the max that can appear anywhere in this list. Three of the shared grid's tracks (the old Owner column, and both blank spacers — the original one, plus the newer one added between the Milestones badge and Plan-dates) are always empty on every row in this view — nothing at any level (Journey, Sub Journey, or a connected scope item) ever puts anything in them — so `.journeys-list { --item-grid-cols: 20px minmax(140px,1.3fr) 10px 90px 10px 190px 10px 115px 100px 116px 120px; }` shrinks those three (160/20/20 → 10/10/10) and spends the reclaimed width on a wider Actions column (56 → 120px), leaving Milestone/Due/Actual/Status at their original widths since a connected item can still expand its own real milestone checklist underneath. (This override predates three later, unrelated column changes to the shared default grid — the Milestones-count badge moving from column 3 to column 4, the old "Actual" placeholder column being removed outright, and the newer blank spacer added between the Milestones badge and Plan-dates — all three applied here too, in step with `--item-grid-cols`' own current 11-column shape; see "Column layout is a real CSS grid" above.)

**A Sub Journey's own "end date" (and start date) is computed live, at render time, from every connected scope item's own milestones — never persisted, never manually editable — exactly what the single-level Journey's own `computeJourneyDateRange()` always did, just renamed to `computeSubJourneyDateRange()` once a level above it needed the name instead.** It flattens every non-`notApplicable` milestone's `dueDate`/`actualDate` across `connectedScopeItems(subJourneyId)` (falling back to a connected item's own `startDate`/`dueDate` when that item has no milestones at all) and returns the earliest/latest as `{startDate, dueDate}`, or `null` if nothing connected has a date to contribute yet. **A top-level Journey's own range is one level further removed — `computeJourneyDateRange(journeyId)` spans the earliest/latest date across every connected Sub Journey's own *already-computed* range** (`connectedSubJourneys(journeyId).map(sj => computeSubJourneyDateRange(sj.id))`), not scope items directly; a Sub Journey that itself has nothing connected contributes nothing (its own range is `null`) rather than dragging the Journey's range towards some default. Neither range is ever persisted onto its own item's `startDate`/`dueDate` the way an ordinary item's computed range is written back at save time (see "Item plan date range roll-up" above) — persisting it would mean hooking every place a connected item's own dates could possibly change (its own milestone edits, a category-template sync, being connected/disconnected) just to keep a cached value from going stale, for no real benefit over just computing it fresh; the same "no persisted state, always fresh at render time" choice Dashboard mode already makes, for the same reason. Both `itemRowHtml()`'s `planDatesHtml` (a computed-badge display, same visual treatment as an ordinary item's own milestone-derived range, showing "—" when nothing connected has a date yet) and the item modal's own Plan-dates section (`updateItemStatusFieldMode()`, branching on `isJourney`/`isSubJourney`) read from whichever of the two functions applies rather than the item's own stored fields.

**Status, like Plan dates, is also computed live — a later, explicit user request** ("Journey should calculate their status based on the status of the included scope items"), reversing this doc's own earlier stance (a plain manual dropdown, since neither level has milestones of its own to roll a status up from) — and follows the identical two-tier shape Plan dates does. `computeSubJourneyStatus(subJourneyId)` is `computedStatusFromMilestones()`'s counterpart for a Sub Journey: the same weakest-link reduce over `STATUS_SEVERITY`, just over `connectedScopeItems(subJourneyId)`'s own already-resolved `status` fields instead of a milestone list — a Sub Journey with any Red-status scope item connected reads Red overall, same "one active problem always surfaces" reasoning; this is exactly what `computeJourneyStatus()` used to do before the Sub Journey level existed, renamed the same way its date-range counterpart was. **`computeJourneyStatus(journeyId)` is one level further removed — the same weakest-link reduce, but over `connectedSubJourneys(journeyId)`'s own already-computed status (each falling back to `'not-started'` if that Sub Journey has nothing connected yet, rather than being skipped)** — so an empty Sub Journey can still legitimately drag its parent Journey's overall status down to "nothing started" (`'not-started'` outranks an active Green/Amber/Red in severity, same order `computedStatusFromMilestones()` uses — see "Item status roll-up" above — so it only drags things down relative to Complete, never relative to a genuinely active problem). Both functions return `null` when nothing is connected yet, same convention as their date-range counterparts; every caller falls back to `'not-started'`, the same neutral default a brand-new item already starts at. Neither is ever persisted onto its own item's `status` field either, for the identical "avoid hooking every place a connected item's status could change" reason the date-range functions aren't — `saveItem()` writes whatever `status` a Journey/Sub Journey already had (or `'not-started'` for a brand-new one) rather than reading the now-hidden `#itemStatusSelect`, and that stored value is simply never read back for display; `itemRowHtml()`'s status badge and `updateItemStatusFieldMode()`'s modal badge both call the appropriate compute function directly instead of `it.status`. `updateItemStatusFieldMode()`'s Status half checks the same `hasMilestones || isJourney || isSubJourney` condition its Plan-dates half already uses, so both fields move in lockstep for either level too, the same way they always have for every other item — the one difference is *which* source each is computed from (`editingMilestones` for a plain item's own checklist, `connectedSubJourneys()`/`computeSubJourneyStatus()` for a Journey, `connectedScopeItems()` directly for a Sub Journey). A brand-new, not-yet-saved Journey or Sub Journey has no id to look up connections by, so both its Status and Plan-dates badges show their respective "nothing yet" fallback (`'not-started'` / "No connected Sub Journeys yet" or "No connected scope items yet", depending on the level) until it's actually saved.

**IT/Business/Budget follow the identical two-tier computed shape — a later, explicit user request** ("journeys should roll-up their IT/Business/Budget from Sub-Journeys... this should not be editable on journey and sub journey level"). See "IT/Business/Budget tags" above for `computeSubJourneyTagStatus()`/`computeJourneyTagStatus()` themselves; the short version is that they're `computeSubJourneyStatus()`/`computeJourneyStatus()`'s own counterparts, one call per RAG3 field, and — unlike Status, which is merely *displayed* as computed while the underlying `status` field stays inert — the tag badges are additionally forced non-clickable on these two item types regardless of role, since `editable` alone (`canManageJourneys()` for a Planner/Editor on these rows) isn't a fine enough signal on its own to gate just this one part of the row.

**`normalizeData()` backfills** all three of `itemType`/`journeyId`/`parentJourneyId` the same way every other item field is self-healed: `itemType` resets to `'scope'` unless it's already exactly `'journey'` or `'subjourney'` (an unrecognized/legacy value falls back safely, same as `status`/`categoryId`) — checked before the `categoryId` repair just below it, since that repair needs to know whether it's dealing with either journey-like type (neither has a category at all) or a plain item. `journeyId` is only ever meaningful on a scope item (`itemType === 'scope'`); it resets to `null` for every other item type outright, and for a scope item resets to `null` unless it resolves to an existing item that's actually `itemType === 'subjourney'` — a reference to a deleted item, or one that's no longer a Sub Journey (including a reference to a *top-level* Journey, which was never a valid target even before the item existed but is now explicitly checked against, not just implicitly excluded), is cleared rather than left dangling, the same orphaned-reference treatment a stale `workstreamId` already gets. `parentJourneyId` mirrors this exactly one level up: only meaningful on a Sub Journey (`itemType === 'subjourney'`), reset to `null` for every other item type, and for a Sub Journey reset to `null` unless it resolves to an existing item that's actually `itemType === 'journey'`.

## Review mode

A review cycle is scoped to exactly one workstream (`activeReviewCycle(wsId)` finds the one cycle for that workstream with both `completedAt` and `cancelledAt` still `null` — `startReviewCycle()` is a no-op if one's already active, so there's never more than one open cycle per workstream at a time). Review mode has no workstream picker of its own any more — it reads the shared `filterWorkstreamId` (see "The shared workstream selector" above), which is guaranteed non-null whenever Scope Item Review specifically is showing (`reviewTab === 'scope'`) — the Action Log tab is the one exception, deliberately usable with no workstream selected at all (see "The shared workstream selector" above and "Action Log" below). The shared sidebar (`renderSidebar()`, `#wsList` — same one Planning and Dashboard use) shows each workstream a small badge regardless of which of the three modes is active: amber "In review" if it has an active cycle, red "Overdue" if `isReviewOverdue(w.id)` (no completed cycle in the last `REVIEW_STALE_DAYS` (30) days, or never reviewed at all), otherwise no badge — so review staleness is visible while planning, not just while reviewing. **`isReviewOverdue(wsId)` first checks whether the workstream has any scope items at all** (`items.some(it => it.workstreamId === wsId)`) **and returns `false` immediately if not** — an empty, just-created workstream has nothing to review yet, so it shouldn't read as "Overdue" from the moment it's added (every item is backfilled with a `startDate`/`dueDate` the instant it's created, by `normalizeData()`, so "no items" and "no items with any dates" are the same condition here — there's no in-between state of an item existing with no dates to check for separately). This guard is shared by both consumers of `isReviewOverdue()` — the sidebar badge and Dashboard's "Overdue for Review" card below.

**Confirmation is milestone-level, not just item-level.** An item with at least one milestone has *no independent stored confirmation at all* — `isItemConfirmedInCycle(cycle, it)` derives it as confirmed only once every one of `it.milestones` has its own entry in `cycle.milestoneConfirmations` (`toggleMilestoneConfirm(cycleId, milestoneId)`, a plain toggle). This mirrors the rest of the app's "computed vs manual" pattern (status, plan dates): there's no separate item-level flag that could drift out of sync with its milestones' actual confirmation state, so "a scope item can only be confirmed once all its milestones are confirmed" is an invariant by construction, not something enforced by a check that could be bypassed. `toggleConfirmAllMilestones(cycleId, itemId)` is a bulk convenience, not a separate source of truth — it confirms every not-yet-confirmed milestone on the item in one click, or un-confirms all of them if every one is already confirmed (same toggle shape). A zero-milestone item still confirms directly via `toggleReviewConfirm(cycleId, itemId)` into `cycle.confirmations`, exactly as it did before milestone-level confirmation existed — there's nothing to derive from. **A confirmation record is created once and only ever flipped, never spliced back out on un-confirm** — `setConfirmRecord()`/`toggleConfirmRecord()` (the shared mechanic behind `toggleReviewConfirm()`/`toggleMilestoneConfirm()`/`toggleConfirmAllMilestones()`) find-or-create the `{itemId|milestoneId, confirmed, updatedAt}` record for a given key and set/flip its `confirmed` flag — a later, explicit user follow-up to the "3 parallel reviewers" question below, since a plain presence/absence array can't carry an `updatedAt` on removal (the record's just gone), which is exactly what made the old shape un-mergeable across two concurrently-edited copies of the same cycle. `setConfirmRecord()` still never materializes a record at all when setting `confirmed:false` on a key with no existing one — a milestone auto-confirmed via its own Complete `status` (see `isMilestoneConfirmedInCycle()`'s own status shortcut, just above) must never pick up a redundant record purely from a bulk "confirm all" pass.

**A milestone (or zero-milestone item) whose own status is already `'complete'` auto-confirms — no manual click needed, with or without an actual entry in `cycle.milestoneConfirmations`/`cycle.confirmations`.** An explicit user request ("mark completed task as reviewed, no need to review them again"): once something is genuinely finished, a reviewer shouldn't have to re-click through it every single review cycle just to satisfy the "everything confirmed" gate — only milestones/items still actively open need attention. `isMilestoneConfirmedInCycle(cycle, m)` (its signature changed from taking a bare `milestoneId` to the whole milestone object, so it can read `m.status`) checks `m.status === 'complete'` *before* falling back to the stored-entry lookup — a milestone finished long before this review cycle even started, and never explicitly clicked in any cycle, still reads as confirmed today. `isItemConfirmedInCycle()`'s own zero-milestone branch gets the same treatment (`it.status === 'complete' || cycle.confirmations.some(...)`). This is deliberately the same shape as the existing `notApplicable` exclusion just below — "this doesn't need a reviewer's attention" — but for a different reason (genuinely done, vs. genuinely out of scope), so it's a separate check, not folded into the `notApplicable` one.

`toggleConfirmAllMilestones()` needed no logic change at all beyond passing the milestone object through — its existing `if (!isMilestoneConfirmedInCycle(cycle, m))` guard, before pushing a real confirmation entry, now naturally skips an already-Complete milestone the exact same way it already skipped an already-manually-confirmed one, so a bulk "confirm all" never writes a redundant entry for one. **Rendering-wise, a Complete milestone/zero-milestone-item gets its own inert, already-checked indicator** (`milestoneRowsHtml()`'s and `itemRowHtml()`'s review-cell branches both gained a `m.status === 'complete'` / `it.status === 'complete'` case, checked before the normal clickable-toggle branch) rather than the clickable circle a still-open, manually-confirmable milestone/item shows — the same "nothing here needs interaction" treatment `notApplicable` milestones already get in this exact column, just with the filled `fa-circle-check` icon instead of `fa-ban` (it *is* confirmed, not excluded). Only the zero-milestone case gets this treatment at the item level — an item *with* milestones keeps its real, clickable "confirm all" bulk button regardless of whether every underlying milestone happens to already be auto-confirmed, since clicking it in that case is just a harmless no-op, not worth a second inert-rendering branch.

**Completion gate, and why it needs no extra bookkeeping for mid-cycle adds.** `canCompleteReviewCycle(cycle)` checks that *every item currently in `cycle.workstreamId`* is confirmed per `isItemConfirmedInCycle()` — it re-derives the live item (and, for each, live milestone) list each call rather than snapshotting anything when the cycle started. This is what makes both "an item added mid-cycle automatically becomes an unconfirmed blocker" *and* "a milestone added mid-cycle to an already-confirmed item un-confirms it" fall out for free (per the product requirement this was built against): there's no separate "what does this cycle cover" list to keep in sync, so a newly-added item or milestone simply has no confirmation entry yet and the `.every()` checks naturally fail until someone confirms it. The flip side: an item (or milestone) *deleted* mid-cycle just stops being checked (its stray confirmation entry, if any, is harmless orphaned data — never pruned, never causes a crash since nothing indexes into `confirmations`/`milestoneConfirmations` by array position).

`completeReviewCycle(cycleId)` re-checks `canCompleteReviewCycle()` itself (defense in depth — the UI already disables the button via the same check, but the guard lives in the mutating function, not just the render), snapshots the confirmation count (below), and sets `completedAt` — permanent once set, a cycle is never un-completed. The "Complete review" button itself reflects `canCompleteReviewCycle()` beyond just the `disabled` attribute — `.btn:disabled` is explicitly dimmed (`opacity:.4`) rather than relying on default/absent browser styling for a custom-colored button, and its `title` explains why it's inert when it is, so a reviewer isn't left wondering whether the button does nothing because of a bug or because something's still unconfirmed.

**A cancelled cycle isn't tracked at all.** `cancelReviewCycle(cycleId)` goes through the confirm modal (as before), but on confirm it splices the cycle out of `reviewCycles` entirely rather than marking a `cancelledAt` and keeping it — a review that got abandoned isn't a record worth keeping, unlike a completed one.
